for a detailed description. The two conjectures are also used in
Brennan and Bresler 2020
. We will take these conjectures as given and state consequences of these hardness results as appropriate in the sequel. Our goal in this section is to cover the motivating examples mentioned in Section
1
through the lens of reductions.

3.1
Tensor principal component analysis with non-Gaussian noise

In Tensor PCA
(
Montanari and Richard 2014
)
, we observe a single order
s
tensor
T(\beta,v)
with dimensions
n^{\otimes s}=\underbrace{n\times\cdots\times n}_{s\text{ times}}
given by

\displaystyle T(\beta,v)=\beta(\sqrt{n}v)^{\otimes s}+\zeta.

(41)

The positive scalar
\beta
denotes the signal-to-noise ratio, the unknown spike
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is typically sampled from a prior distribution, and the observations are corrupted by a symmetric Gaussian noise tensor
\zeta
. Specifically, let
G\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes n^{\otimes s}}
be an asymmetric tensor with entries that are
\mathsf{i.i.d.}

\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, and define the symmetrized noise tensor
\zeta
by

\displaystyle\zeta_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}=\frac{1}{s!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{s}}G_{i_{\pi(1)},\dots,i_{\pi(s)}}\quad\text{for all }(i_{1},\dots,i_{s})\in[n]^{s}.

(42)

For notational convenience, let
\sigma_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}^{2}
denote the variance of
\zeta_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}
. Since
s
is a constant, all entries of
\zeta
have constant-order variance. In particular, the minimum variance is
\sigma_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}^{2}=1/s!
when all indices
(i_{1},\dots,i_{s})
are distinct, while the maximum variance is
\sigma_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}^{2}=1
when all indices are identical.

The task of recovering
v
to within nontrivial
\ell_{2}
-error
o(1)
exhibits a statistical-computational gap. On the one hand, it is information-theoretically possible to recover
v
when
\beta=\widetilde{\omega}(n^{(1-s)/2})
via exhaustive search
(
Montanari and Richard 2014
;
Lesieur et al. 2017b
;
Chen 2019
;
Jagannath et al. 2020
;
Perry et al. 2020
)
. On the other hand, all known polynomial-time algorithms require a stronger signal, namely
\beta=\widetilde{\Omega}(n^{-s/4})
, including spectral methods
(
Montanari and Richard 2014
)
, the sum-of-squares hierarchy
(
Hopkins et al. 2015
;
Hopkins et al. 2017
)
, and spectral algorithms based on the Kikuchi hierarchy
(
Wein et al. 2019
)
. This statistical-computational gap in tensor PCA is believed to be fundamental, and the following reduction-based computational hardness result from
Brennan and Bresler 2020
makes this precise.

Proposition 2
.

Let
n
be a parameter and
s\geq 3
be a constant. Let
\zeta\in\real^{n^{\otimes s}}
be the symmetric Gaussian tensor defined in Eq. (
42
) and define

\displaystyle T=\beta(\sqrt{n}v)^{\otimes s}+\zeta\quad\text{where}\quad v\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1}).

(43)

Suppose the
k
-
\mathrm{HPC}^{s}
conjecture holds. If
\beta=\widetilde{o}\big(n^{-s/4}\big)
and
\beta=\omega\big(n^{-s/2}\sqrt{s\log(n)}\big)
, then there
cannot
exist an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\real^{n^{\otimes s}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
that runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathcal{A}(T),v\big\rangle=\Omega(1)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad\Omega_{n}(1).

(44)

A few comments on Proposition
2
are in order. First, the condition
\beta=\omega\big(n^{-s/2}\sqrt{s\log(n)}\big)
is not an essential feature of the result and can be ignored, since it is weaker than the information-theoretic threshold
\beta=\widetilde{\omega}(n^{(1-s)/2})
necessary for recovering
v
with constant probability
(
Montanari and Richard 2014
;
Chen 2019
)
.
Second, we remark that the requirement

\big\langle\mathcal{A}(T),v\big\rangle=\Omega(1)

is weaker than the condition

\big\|\mathcal{A}(T)-v\big\|_{2}=o(1).

Thus, any estimation algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that achieves
\ell_{2}
error
o(1)
automatically satisfies requirement (
44
). Third, Proposition
2
as stated differs from the results of
Brennan and Bresler 2020
in two ways: In their setting, (i) the spike
\sqrt{n}v
is a discrete vector in
\{-1,1\}^{n}
, and (ii) the Gaussian noise
\zeta
is asymmetric. Nevertheless, the model we consider exhibits the same gap as the model in
Brennan and Bresler 2020
because there is an exact reduction from the Tensor PCA model in
Brennan and Bresler 2020
to our model (
41
). This reduction proceeds by multiplying a uniformly random rotation matrix to each mode of the tensor, and achieves zero total variation deficiency. For completeness, we provide the proof of this claim in Appendix
B.5
and a
formal proof of Proposition
2
in Section
8.1
.

The key takeaway from Proposition
2
is that computational hardness result holds under the Gaussian noise model. This restriction is somewhat unsatisfactory, as real-world observations can be affected by various types of non-Gaussian noise—indeed, this has also been noticed in recent work
(
Kunisky 2025
)
. We now use our results from Section
2
to extend reduction-based hardness results for tensor PCA to a larger class of non-Gaussian distributions.

To be concrete, consider the same tensor PCA model as in Eq. (
41
), but with observations corrupted by general non-Gaussian noise. Specifically, we study the following model:

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}(\beta,v)=\beta(\sqrt{n}v)^{\otimes s}+\widetilde{\zeta},

(45)

Here the noise tensor
\widetilde{\zeta}\in\real^{n^{\otimes s}}
is still symmetric—specifically, let
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
denote the distribution of the noise, then
the entries
\widetilde{\zeta}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}\sim\mathcal{Q}_{0}
and satisfy
\widetilde{\zeta}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}=\widetilde{\zeta}_{i_{\pi(1)},\dots,i_{\pi(s)}}
for any permutation
\pi
on
[s]
. We write the density of the noise distribution
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
as

\displaystyle f_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}(y)=\frac{1}{\tau}\exp\big(-\psi\big(t/\tau\big)\big)\quad\text{for all }y\in\real,

(46)

where
\tau>0
is the scale parameter of distribution
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
and
\psi:\real\rightarrow\real
is a general function. Recall the definitions of
\mathsf{Err}(\psi,2N,\lambda)
in Eq. (
25
),
M(\psi,2N,\lambda)
in Eq. (
26
), as well as the evaluation time
T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N)
from Definition
1
. We will make use of the signed kernel
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}
(
14
), with target density defined in Eq. (
20
).
We make the following assumption on the function
\psi
.

Assumption 4
.

The function
\psi
satisfies Assumption
2
with a pair of constants
(C_{\psi},\widetilde{C}_{\psi})
. For each
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, if the pair of parameters
(N,\lambda)
are set to

N=\Theta\Big(\overline{C}_{\psi}\log^{(2(1+C_{\psi}))}\big(3\overline{C}_{\psi}/\epsilon\big)\Big)\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda=\Theta\Big(\mathsf{polylog}\big(1/\epsilon\big)\Big),

\text{then}\quad\mathsf{Err}(\psi,2N,\lambda)\leq\epsilon/6,\quad\quad M(\psi,2N,\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(1),\quad\text{and}\quad T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N)=\mathcal{O}\big(\mathsf{poly}(1/\epsilon)\big).

Note that a broad class of noise distributions satisfies Assumption
4
. As illustrated in Corollary
2
, Corollary
1
, and Example
1
, this class includes the generalized normal distribution
\mathsf{GN}(0,\tau,\beta)
, which has lighter tails than the Gaussian distribution, as well as the Student’s t-distribution (including the Cauchy distribution), which has heavier tails. It also includes any distribution whose negative log-density
\psi
is a globally analytic function—such as the logistic distribution, the hyperbolic secant distribution, etc. We are now ready to state the result that relates the Gaussian noise model (
41
) to the general noise model (
45
).

Theorem 3
.

Let
n
be a parameter and
s\geq 3
be a constant. Let
\beta\in\real
denote the signal strength and
v\in\real^{n}
denote the spike vector. Let tensor
T(\beta,v)\in\real^{n^{\otimes s}}
be distributed according to model (
41
). Let tensor
\widetilde{T}(\beta,v)\in\real^{n^{\otimes s}}
be distributed according to model (
45
) with noise distribution
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
. Suppose Assumption
4
holds. Then there is a reduction algorithm
\mathcal{R}:\real^{n^{\otimes s}}\rightarrow\real^{n^{\otimes s}}
satisfying the following:
For each
\delta\in(0,1)
, if the scale of
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
is set to
\tau^{2}=\Theta\big(\mathsf{polylog}(n^{s}/\delta)\big)
, then
\mathcal{R}
runs in time

\mathcal{O}\Big(\mathsf{poly}\big(n^{s}/\delta\big)\Big)\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{\beta\in\real,v\in\real^{n}}\;\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathcal{R}\big(T(\beta,v)\big),\widetilde{T}(\beta,v)\Big)\leq\delta.

We provide the proof of Theorem
3
in Section
8.2
. A few comments on Theorem
3
are in order. First, the reduction guarantee does not impose any assumptions on the signal strength
\beta
or the spike vector
v
. In particular, there is no need to assume any prior distribution on
\beta
or
v
. Second, the reduction algorithm
\mathcal{R}
is constructed by applying the entrywise transformation
\mathsf{K}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
from Theorem
1
to each entry of the tensor
T(\beta,v)
. Specifically, recall that the distribution
\mathcal{Q}_{\theta}
defined in Eq. (
20
) is equivalent to a location model centered at
\theta
, but with noise distribution
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
. Then, conditional on
\beta
and
v
, for each index tuple
(i_{1},\dots,i_{s})
, we have

T(\beta,v)_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta,\sigma^{2}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}})\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{T}(\beta,v)_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}\sim\mathcal{Q}_{\theta},\quad\text{where }\theta=\beta n^{s/2}v_{i_{1}}\times v_{i_{2}}\times\cdots\times v_{i_{s}}.

This setup exactly matches the source and target distributions considered in Theorem
1
.

The following hardness result immediately follows by combing the hardness of the source problem (Proposition
2
) with the reduction (Theorem
3
).

Corollary 5
.

Suppose
s\geq 3
is a constant. Consider the tensor
\widetilde{T}=\beta(\sqrt{n}v)^{\otimes n}+\widetilde{\zeta}
, where
v\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1})
and
\widetilde{\zeta}\in\real^{n^{\otimes s}}
is the symmetric noise tensor whose entries have density
f_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
defined in Eq. (
46
). Suppose the noise distribution
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
satisfies Assumption
4
and the scale parameter satisfies
\tau^{2}=\Theta\big(\mathsf{polylog}(n)\big)
. Suppose the
k
-
\mathrm{HPC}^{s}
conjecture holds. If
\beta=\widetilde{o}\big(n^{-s/4}\big)
and
\beta=\omega\big(n^{-s/2}\sqrt{s\log(n)}\big)
, then there
cannot
exist an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\real^{n^{\otimes s}}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
that runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\big\langle\mathcal{A}\big(\widetilde{T}\big),v\big\rangle=\Omega(1)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad\Omega_{n}(1).

We prove Corollary
5
in Section
8.3
. A few comments on Corollary
5
are in order. First, note that the scale parameter (which roughly corresponds to variance inflation)
\tau^{2}=\Theta\big(\mathsf{polylog}(n)\big)
only affects the statistical-computational gap up to a polylogarithmic factor. This is because the signal-to-noise ratio in the non-Gaussian tensor PCA model (
45
) is given by
\beta/\tau
, and we only require
\tau
to be
polylogarithmic
in
n
. Note that the gap itself is polynomial, so this polylogarithmic change should not be thought of as substantial. Second, to the best of our knowledge, Corollary
5
is the first reduction-based hardness result for tensor PCA with non-Gaussian noise. It demonstrates a striking universality phenomenon: the same computational hardness persists for a wide class of noise distributions, including both light-tailed (e.g., generalized normal) and heavy-tailed (e.g., Student’s
t
or Cauchy) distributions.

Remark 4
.

While
Kunisky 2025
also establish a form of universality, their result applies only to a restricted class of algorithms, namely low coordinate degree algorithms. In contrast, our result applies to all polynomial-time algorithms, a strictly broader class. Similarly,
Brennan et al. 2019
demonstrate universality of computational lower bounds for the submatrix model; however, their techniques apply only when the matrix entries are drawn from at most two distributions. In contrast, our results hold under a continuous prior on the rank-one tensor, so that the entries of the tensor may be drawn from infinitely many possible distributions.

3.2
Symmetric mixtures of linear regressions and generalized linear models

In the symmetric version of the mixture of linear regressions model
(
Quandt and Ramsey 1978
;
Städler et al. 2010
)
, there is some unknown vector
v\in\real^{d}
and we observe
\mathsf{i.i.d.}
data
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
generated according to

\displaystyle y_{i}=R_{i}\cdot\langle x_{i},v\rangle+\zeta_{i},\quad\text{where}\quad\zeta_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(47)

Here, the random variable
R_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
and the noise
\zeta_{i}
are unobserved and independent of each other. For convenience, we denote
\eta=\|v\|_{2}
. In the setting that
v\in\real^{d}
is
k
-sparse, the task is of estimating
v
in
\ell_{2}
-error
o(\eta)
exhibits a statistical-computational gap. In particular, the information-theoretic limit occurs at sample complexity
n=\widetilde{\Theta}(k\log(d)/\eta^{4})
; see
Fan et al. 2018
for details
4
4

4

Although the information-theoretic lower bound in
Fan et al. 2018
is stated for a hypothesis testing problem, it also applies to the estimation setting, since any estimator achieving
\ell_{2}
-error
o(\eta)
can be used to solve the corresponding testing problem; see
Brennan and Bresler 2020
for a rigorous argument.
. On the other hand, there is a computational lower bound of
n=\widetilde{\Theta}(k^{2}/\eta^{4})
, below which no polynomial-time algorithm can succeed at the estimation task, under the
k
-
\mathrm{BPC}
conjecture; see
Brennan and Bresler 2020

for the precise statement. A refined computational lower bound—along with runtime tradeoffs—was obtained by
Arpino and Venkataramanan 2023
for the class of low-degree polynomial algorithms. We restate the reduction-based hardness result of
Brennan and Bresler 2020
as the following proposition.

Proposition 3
.

Let
k,d,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\eta>0
be polynomial in each other,
k=o(\sqrt{d})
, and
k=o(n^{1/6})
. Consider the setting
x_{i}\overset{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
,
\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathsf{Rad}
,
v=\eta\bm{1}_{S}/\sqrt{k}
for some
k
-subset
S\subseteq[d]
, and
y_{i}
follows model (
47
) for all
i\in[n]
. Suppose the
k
-
\mathrm{BPC}
conjecture holds. If
n=\widetilde{o}\big(k^{2}/\eta^{4}\big)
, then there cannot exist an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:(\real^{d}\times\real)^{n}\rightarrow\real^{d}
that runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\big\|\mathcal{A}\big(\{x_{i},y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\big)-v\big\|_{2}=o(\eta)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad 1-o_{n}(1).

While Proposition
3
imposes the constraint
k=o(\sqrt{d})
and
k=o(n^{1/6})
on the sparsity level for technical reasons, it still establishes computational hardness in a nontrivial regime: By reparametrizing with

\displaystyle k=\widetilde{\Theta}\big(n^{\beta}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\eta^{4}=\widetilde{\Theta}\big(n^{-\alpha}\big),

(48)

for constants
\beta\in(0,1)
and
\alpha>0
, we obtain that every instance in the regime

\displaystyle\big\{(\alpha,\beta):0<\beta\leq 1/6,\;1-2\beta\leq\alpha\leq 1-\beta\big\}

(49)

is statistically possible but remains computationally intractable for estimation; see Figure
3
for an illustration.

At a heuristic level, the
k^{2}
computational lower bound arises because in the model (
47
),
the Rademacher random variable
R_{i}
destroys information about the sign of the regression term
\langle x_{i},v\rangle
. Contrast this with the case of
sparse linear regression, where no such statistical-computational gap appears. This observation naturally raises the following question: Does the
\Omega(k^{2})
computational lower bound arise for any signless observation model in variants of sparse linear regression?

Toward answering this question, we consider the class of generalized linear models
(
Barbier et al. 2019
)
with even link functions. Specifically, suppose there is an unknown vector
v\in\real^{d}
and we observe
\mathsf{i.i.d.}
data
\{(x_{i},\widetilde{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
generated according to

\displaystyle\widetilde{y}_{i}=f\big(\langle x_{i},v\rangle/\tau\big)+\widetilde{\zeta}_{i},\quad\text{where}\quad\widetilde{\zeta}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(50)

In the above,
f:\real\rightarrow\real
is a general even link function and the parameter
\tau>0
is a proxy for the signal-to-noise ratio, which will be specified in concrete applications. Also suppose that the signal
v
is
k
-sparse.
We now use our results from Section
2
to extend reduction-based hardness results for mixture of sparse linear regressions to a class of generalized sparse linear models.

We make the following assumption on the link function
f
. Recall the definitions of
L_{f}(2N)
and
\widetilde{L}_{f}(2N,\tau)
in Eq. (
35
) as well as the evaluation time
T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N)
from Definition
1
. We will make use of the signed kernel
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}
(
14
), with target density defined in Eq. (
33
).

Assumption 5
.

The link function
f:\real\rightarrow\real
is even, i.e.,
f(x)=f(-x)
for all
x\in\real
, and it satisfies Assumption
3
with a pair of constants
(C_{f},\widetilde{C}_{f})
. For each
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, if
N=\Theta\Big(\overline{C}_{f}\log^{2(1+C_{f})}\big(4\overline{C}_{f}/\epsilon\big)\Big)
where
\overline{C}_{f}
is defined in Eq. (
35
), then we have for some
\tau=\mathcal{O}\big(\mathsf{polylog}(1/\epsilon)\big)
,

L_{f}(2N)=\mathcal{O}\Big(\mathsf{polylog}(1/\epsilon)\Big),\quad\widetilde{L}_{f}(2N,\tau)=\mathcal{O}\Big(\mathsf{polylog}(1/\epsilon)\Big),\quad\text{and}\quad T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N)=\mathcal{O}\Big(\mathsf{poly}(1/\epsilon)\Big).

Note that a broad class of functions satisfies Assumption
5
. As illustrated in Corollary
3
, Corollary
4
, and Example
2
, this includes monomials of constant degree—for instance, the function
f(\theta)=\theta^{2}
is covered, which corresponds to the phase retrieval model in the context of generalized linear models. Moreover, Assumption
5
also encompasses the class of globally analytic functions (see Definition
2
), which includes many canonical and practically relevant link functions such as
f(\theta)=\cos(\theta)
,
f(\theta)=\mathsf{sech}(\theta)
, and
f(\theta)=(1+\theta^{2})^{-1}
, among others. Additionally, by Lemma
2
, any linear combination of these basic functions is also covered. We are now ready to relate mixtures of linear regressions (
47
) to the generalized linear model (
50
) with a link function satisfying Assumption
5
.

Theorem 4
.

Let
v\in\real^{d}
denote the unknown vector and let
\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq\{-1,1\}^{n}
be any vector of signs. Conditioned on
\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
v
,
suppose samples
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
are drawn
\mathsf{i.i.d.}
according to model (
47
) and samples
\{(x_{i},\widetilde{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
are drawn
\mathsf{i.i.d.}
according to model (
50
).
Suppose the link function
f:\real\rightarrow\real
satisfies Assumption
5
. Then there is a reduction algorithm
\mathcal{R}:(\real^{d}\times\real)^{n}\rightarrow(\real^{d}\times\real)^{n}
satisfying the following:
For each
\delta\in(0,1)
, if we set

\displaystyle\tau=\mathsf{polylog}(n/\delta)\quad\text{and large enough so that}\quad\tau\geq 2\max_{i\in[n]}\;\big|\langle x_{i},v\rangle\big|,

(51)

then the reduction
\mathcal{R}
runs in time

\mathcal{O}\Big(\mathsf{poly}(n/\delta)\Big)\quad\text{and satisfies}\quad\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathcal{R}\big(\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\big),\{(x_{i},\widetilde{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\Big)\leq\delta.

We provide the proof in Section
8.4
. Note that the reduction guarantee does not impose any assumptions on the sign variables
\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and the covariates
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. It also does not place any structural assumptions on
v
such as sparsity. In particular, there is no need to assume any prior distribution on
\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, and
v
. This is because the reduction algorithm
\mathcal{R}
is constructed by applying the entrywise transformation
\mathsf{K}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
from Theorem
2
to each sample
y_{i}
. Specifically, conditional on the tuple
(R_{i},x_{i},v)
, we have

y_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta,1)\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{y}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(f(\theta/\tau),1\big),\quad\text{where }\theta=R_{i}\cdot\langle x_{i},v\rangle.

This setup exactly matches the source and target distributions considered in Theorem
2
.

Remark 5
.

Note that any instance of the mixture of sparse linear regressions (
\mathsf{MixSLR}
) (
47
) with signal
v
is mapped under the reduction
\mathcal{R}
to an instance of the sparse generalized linear model (
\mathsf{SpGLM}
) (
50
) with signal
v/\tau
. In most applications of Theorem
4
, it suffices to take
\delta
to be an inverse polynomial of
n
and in the Gaussian covariance setting
x_{i}\overset{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, standard Gaussian tail bounds and a union bound yield

\mathbb{P}\Big\{\max_{i\in[n]}|\langle x_{i},v\rangle|\leq 2\sqrt{\log(n)}\Big\}\geq 1-n^{-1}\quad\text{for}\quad\|v\|_{2}\leq 1.

Thus, condition (
51
) is satisfied by letting
\tau=\Theta(\mathsf{polylog}(n))
. Therefore, the reduction
\mathcal{R}
preserves the sparsity
k
and only changes the signal strength
\eta
by a polylogarithmic factor.

Figure 3
:
Phase diagrams for the mixture of sparse linear regressions (
\mathsf{MixSLR}
; panel (a)) and sparse phase retrieval (
\mathsf{SPR}
; panel (b)), under the parameterization
k=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{\beta})
and
\eta^{4}=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{-\alpha})
, with constants
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
.
The purple region denotes the statistically impossible regime; the red region denotes the statistically possible but computationally intractable regime; the green region denotes the computationally tractable regime; and the black region denotes a statistically possible yet conjectured computationally hard regime, whose proof remains open.
The reduction
\mathcal{R}
maps
\mathsf{MixSLR}
instances with parameters
(\alpha,\beta)
to
\mathsf{SPR}
instances with the same
(\alpha,\beta)
for all
(\alpha,\beta)
in the phase diagram, so any computational hardness regime established in panel (a) transfers directly to panel (b).

The hardness result of
\mathsf{MixSLR}
(Proposition
3
) and the reduction (Theorem
4
) imply the following computational hardness of
\mathsf{SpGLM}
.

Corollary 6
.

Let
k,d,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\eta>0
be polynomial in each other,
k=o(\sqrt{d})
, and
k=o(n^{1/6})
. Consider the setting
x_{i}\overset{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
,
v=\eta\bm{1}_{S}/\sqrt{k}
for some
k
-subset
S\subseteq[d]
, and
\widetilde{y}_{i}
follows model (
50
) for all
i\in[n]
. Suppose in model (
50
), the link function
f
satisfies Assumption
5
and the scale parameter satisfies
\tau=\Theta\big(\mathsf{polylog}(n)\big)
. Suppose the
k
-
\mathrm{BPC}
conjecture holds. If
n=\widetilde{o}\big(k^{2}/\eta^{4}\big)
, then there cannot exist an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:(\real^{d}\times\real)^{n}\rightarrow\real^{d}
that runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\big\|\mathcal{A}\big(\{x_{i},\widetilde{y}_{i}\}\big)-v\big\|_{2}=o(\eta)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad 1-o_{n}(1).

We provide the proof of Corollary
6
in Section
8.5
, and record a few remarks here.
First, the constraints
k=o(\sqrt{d})
and
k=o(n^{1/6})
are inherited from Proposition
3
, which is the source of the computational hardness. Nevertheless, this still establishes computational hardness in the nontrivial regime defined in Eq. (
49
) under the parameterization (
48
); see the illustration of this computationally intractable regime in Figure
3
. Second, our reduction itself imposes no constraints on
k
, so any hardness shown for
\mathsf{MixSLR}
automatically transfers to
\mathsf{SpGLM}
. In particular, to understand the computational complexity of
\mathsf{SpGLM}
in the black region of Figure
3
(b), it suffices to understand the corresponding black region of Figure
3
(a).
Third, the scaling factor
\tau
does not affect the signal strength beyond a polylogarithmic factor, since in model (
50
) the effective signal strength is
\|v\|_{2}/\tau=\eta/\tau
, and we set
\tau=\Theta(\mathsf{polylog}(n))
.
We have thus demonstrated that the
\widetilde{\Omega}(k^{2})
computational barrier is universal for models with signless observations arising from even link functions. This resolves an open question posed in
Brennan and Bresler 2020
. We remark that a
k
-to-
k^{2}
gap has been established under the statistical query model
(
Wang et al. 2019
)
for a class of even link functions, but this only applies to a subset of algorithms and link functions.

We now highlight the computational hardness result for sparse phase retrieval (
\mathsf{SPR}
), where we have
the model

\displaystyle\widetilde{y}_{i}=|\langle x_{i},v\rangle|^{2}+\widetilde{\zeta}_{i},\quad\text{where}\quad\widetilde{\zeta}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(52)

The following computational hardness result immediately follows from Corollary
6
.

Corollary 7

(Sparse Phase Retrieval)
.

Let
k,d,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\eta>0
be polynomial in each other,
k=o(\sqrt{d})
, and
k=o(n^{1/6})
. Consider the setting
x_{i}\overset{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
,
v=\eta\bm{1}_{S}/\sqrt{k}
for some
k
-subset
S\subseteq[d]
, and
\widetilde{y}_{i}
follows the phase retrieval model (
52
) for all
i\in[n]
. Suppose the
k
-
\mathrm{BPC}
conjecture holds.
If
n=\widetilde{o}\big(k^{2}/\eta^{4}\big)
, then there cannot exist an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:(\real^{d}\times\real)^{n}\rightarrow\real^{d}
that runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\big\|\mathcal{A}\big(\{x_{i},\widetilde{y}_{i}\}\big)-v\big\|_{2}=o(\eta)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad 1-o_{n}(1).

Note that the square link function
f(t)=t^{2}
satisfies Assumption
5
(see Corollary
3
).
Hence, Corollary
7
follows directly from Corollary
6
by setting
f(\theta)=\theta^{2}
and absorbing
\tau
into
\eta
.
The information-theoretic limit for
k
-sparse phase retrieval is known to occur at sample complexity
n=\Theta\big(k\log(d)/\eta^{4}\big)
; see
Cai et al. 2016
;
Lecué and Mendelson 2015
. In contrast, all polynomial-time algorithms require sample complexity in the order of
k^{2}
; see
Li and Voroninski 2013
;
Cai et al. 2016
;
Wang et al. 2017
;
Celentano et al. 2020
. Motiveted by this gap,
Cai et al. 2016
conjectured that any computationally efficient method must require
n=\widetilde{\Omega}(k^{2}/\eta^{4})
samples. Corollary
4
provides the first reduction-based confirmation of this conjecture by establishing a computational lower bound: no polynomial-time algorithm can succeed when
n=\widetilde{o}(k^{2}/\eta^{4})
. This demonstrates a statistical–computational gap between the information-theoretic rate
k
and the computational barrier
k^{2}
. In particular, it establishes computational hardness in a nontrivial regime; see Figure
3
(b) for an illustration.

3.3
Sparse rank-1 submatrix model and planted submatrix model

In the sparse rank-1 submatrix model (
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
), we observe an
n\times n
matrix

\displaystyle M=\mu\,rc^{\top}+\zeta,\quad\text{where }r,c\in S_{n,k},\;\zeta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes n\times n}.

(53)

Here,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
is the signal strength,
\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes n\times n}
denotes the distribution of an
n\times n
matrix with
\mathsf{i.i.d.}

\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries, and
S_{n,k}
denotes set of
k
-sparse unit vectors defined as

\displaystyle S_{n,k}:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}:\;\|x\|_{0}=k,\ x_{i}\in\left\{0,\pm\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\right\}\text{ for all }i\in[n]\right\}.

(54)

The corresponding detection problem can be formalized as distinguishing between the following two hypotheses upon observing the matrix
M
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{0}:M\text{ follows model~\eqref{eq:rank-1-model} with }\mu=0,\quad\text{and}

(55a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{1}:M\text{ follows model~\eqref{eq:rank-1-model} for some }\mu>0,r,c\in S_{n,k}.

(55b)

The detection problem in Eq. (
55
) exhibits a computational barrier, which we briefly describe. Define the computational threshold

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathsf{Rank1}}^{\text{comp}}:=\min\big\{k,\sqrt{n}\big\}.

(56)

When the signal strength satisfies
\mu=\widetilde{\omega}\big(\mu_{\mathsf{Rank1}}^{\text{comp}}\big)
, polynomial-time algorithms exist for detection, and these also succeed for recovering
r,c
under
H_{1}
. Specifically, if
\mu=\widetilde{\omega}(k)
, then each entry of the signal matrix
\mu\,rc^{\top}
dominates the noise
\zeta
. If
\mu=\widetilde{\omega}(\sqrt{n})
, then spectral methods succeed
(
Montanari et al. 2015
)
, since the spectral norm of the noise matrix is only
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})
and the top singular vectors of
M
therefore reveal information about the signal
\mu\,rc^{\top}
.
Conversely, it has been shown via reductions from the planted clique conjecture that no polynomial-time algorithm can succeed when
\mu=\widetilde{o}\big(\mu_{\mathsf{Rank1}}^{\text{comp}}\big)
; see
Brennan et al. 2018
.

Figure 4
:
Phase diagrams for the sparse rank-1 submatrix model (
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
; panel (a)) and the planted submatrix model (
\mathsf{PlantSubMat}
; panel (b)), under the parameterization
\mu/k=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{-\alpha})
and
k=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{\beta})
for
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
. The purple region indicates the statistically impossible regime. The green region corresponds to the computationally tractable regime where polynomial-time algorithms are known to succeed. The red region indicates the statistically possible but computationally intractable regime. In panel (a), the computational threshold is given by the line
\alpha=\beta-1/2
, while in panel (b), the threshold is the line
\alpha=2\beta-1
. The reduction
\mathcal{R}
established in Theorem
5
maps instances with parameters
(\alpha,\beta)
in
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
to instances with parameters
(2\alpha,\beta)
in
\mathsf{PlantSubMat}
. This transformation demonstrates a pointwise correspondence between the phase diagrams, preserving the computational tractability and intractability of problem instances.

In the planted submatrix model
(\mathsf{PlantSubMat})
, we observe an
n\times n
matrix

\displaystyle M=\mu\,rc^{\top}+\zeta,\quad\text{where }r,c\in S_{n,k}^{+},\;\zeta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes n\times n}.

(57)

Here, the spike vectors
r
and
c
are restricted to have nonnegative entries. Specifically,
S_{n,k}^{+}
denotes the set of
k
-sparse unit vectors with nonnegative coordinates:

\displaystyle S_{n,k}^{+}\;:=\;\left\{x\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}:\|x\|_{0}=k,\;x_{i}\in\left\{0,\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\right\}\ \forall i\in[n]\right\}.

(58)

The corresponding detection problem involves distinguishing between the following hypotheses upon observing
M
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{0}:M\text{ follows model~\eqref{eq:plantedmat} with }\mu=0,\quad\text{and}

(59a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{1}:M\text{ follows model~\eqref{eq:plantedmat} for some }\mu>0,r,c\in S_{n,k}^{+}.

(59b)

Compared to the sparse rank-1 submatrix model, the planted submatrix model exhibits a different computational threshold, defined as

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathsf{Plant}}^{\text{comp}}:=\min\left\{k,\tfrac{n}{k}\right\}.

(60)

When
\mu=\widetilde{\omega}\big(\mu_{\mathsf{Plant}}^{\text{comp}}\big)
, polynomial-time algorithms exist. For instance, when
\mu=\widetilde{\omega}(k)
, each entry of the signal matrix
\mu\,rc^{\top}
again dominates the noise. Alternatively, when
\mu=\widetilde{\omega}(n/k)
, summing all entries of
\mu\,rc^{\top}
yields a signal
\mu k=\widetilde{\omega}(n)
, while the total sum of entries in the noise matrix is only
\widetilde{O}(n)
, making the two hypotheses distinguishable. Conversely, when
\mu=\widetilde{o}\big(\mu_{\mathsf{Plant}}^{\text{comp}}\big)
, then no polynomial-time algorithm is known, and evidence of computational hardness has been provided through reductions from the planted clique conjecture
(
Ma and Wu 2015
;
Brennan et al. 2018
)
.

As observed, the computational threshold
\mu_{\mathsf{Plant}}^{\mathrm{comp}}
is significantly smaller than
\mu_{\mathsf{Rank1}}^{\mathrm{comp}}
in the regime
k\geq\sqrt{n}
, indicating that
\mathsf{PlantSubMat}
is computationally easier than
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
. To rigorously establish this relation,
we construct a reduction from
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
to
\mathsf{PlantSubMat}
, thereby showing that the computational threshold of
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
directly implies the corresponding threshold of
\mathsf{PlantSubMat}
, without relying on the planted clique conjecture. We now formally state the result.

Theorem 5
.

Let
S_{n,k}\subseteq\real^{n}
be defined as in Eq. (
54
). There exists a universal and positive constant
C
and a reduction algorithm
\mathcal{R}:\real^{n\times n}\rightarrow\real^{n\times n}
such that the following holds: For each
\delta\in(0,1)
, if the tuple of parameters
(\mu,k,n,\tau)
satisfies

\displaystyle\tau^{2}\geq C\log^{24}(n^{2}/\delta)+\frac{4\mu^{2}}{k^{2}},

(61)

then the reduction
\mathcal{R}
runs in time
\mathcal{O}\Big(n^{2}\log^{64}\big(Cn^{2}/\delta\big)\Big)
and

\displaystyle\sup_{\mu\in\real,\;r,c\in S_{n,k}}\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\bigg(\mathcal{R}\Big(\mu rc^{\top}+\zeta\Big),\;\frac{\mu^{2}}{\tau^{2}k}|r||c|^{\top}+\zeta\bigg)\leq\delta,

where
\zeta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes n\times n}
and
|r|,|c|\in\real^{n}
denote the vectors obtained by taking the entrywise absolute value of
r,c
, respectively.

We provide the proof of Theorem
5
in Section
8.6
. Note that it is typical to set
\delta=\mathcal{O}(n^{-C^{\prime}})
for some constant
C^{\prime}>0
to achieve meaningful reductions, so that condition (
61
) is satisfied by choosing
\tau=\Theta(\mathrm{polylog}(n))
.

Let us now discuss some consequences of the theorem, focusing on the regime where
\mu/k=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{-\alpha})
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
. The reduction maps an instance of
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
with parameters
(\mu,k,n)
to an instance of
\mathsf{PlantSubMat}
with parameters
\big(\mu^{2}/(\tau^{2}k),\,k,\,n\big)
. Since
\tau=\Theta(\mathrm{polylog}(n))
, its effect on the signal strength can be neglected. This ensures a pointwise correspondence between the phase diagrams of the two models: if the
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
instance with parameters
(\mu,k,n)
is computationally tractable, then its image under the reduction, an instance of
\mathsf{PlantSubMat}
with parameters
(\mu^{2}/(\tau^{2}k),k,n)
, is also tractable, and the same holds for intractability.
This correspondence can be formalized by parameterizing
\mu/k=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{-\alpha})
and
k=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{\beta})
for
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
. Under this parameterization, a point
(\alpha,\beta)
in the
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
phase diagram is mapped to
(2\alpha,\beta)
in the
\mathsf{PlantSubMat}
phase diagram, confirming that the reduction preserves computational feasibility pointwise; see Figure
4
for an illustration.

Finally, we emphasize that Theorem
5
establishes computational lower bounds without relying on the planted clique conjecture. Indeed, even if the planted clique conjecture were false, a computational lower bound for
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
would still imply the corresponding lower bound for
\mathsf{PlantSubMat}
.
Moreover, reductions from
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
provide evidence of hardness beyond polynomial time. Since existing hardness results for
\mathsf{PlantSubMat}
are conditioned on the planted clique conjecture
(
Brennan et al. 2018
;
Ma and Wu 2015
)
, they rule out only polynomial-time algorithms. However, these reductions lose strength once we go beyond polynomial time, since the planted clique problem is known to be solvable in quasi-polynomial time, specifically
\mathcal{O}\!\left(n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}\right)
, whenever the clique size exceeds the information-theoretic threshold. In contrast, reductions from
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
remain meaningful in this regime, since
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
is believed to require subexponential time in the hard regime. More concretely, if we fix
\mu=\widetilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{n})
and let the sparsity ratio be
\rho=k/n
, then in the statistically feasible but computationally intractable regime
n^{-1/2}\ll\rho\ll 1
,
Ding et al. 2024
showed that recovery
of the spike requires time
\mathcal{O}\!\left(\exp(\rho^{2}n)\right)
, and evidence for the optimality of this recovery algorithm was also provided. Consequently, hardness based on
\mathsf{Rank1SubMat}
can exclude a strictly larger class of algorithms than hardness based on planted clique.

4
Discussion

Motivated by the goal of characterizing fundamental statistical-computational tradeoffs in canonical high-dimensional models, we constructed a Markov kernel that maps the Gaussian location model (the source) to general target models within small total variation deficiency. The target models we covered include (i) general non-Gaussian location models and (ii) Gaussian models with a nonlinearly transformed location parameter. Building on this kernel, we designed a computationally efficient reduction that transforms a single source observation into a single target observation without knowledge of the underlying location parameter. This single-sample reduction allows us to establish new computational lower bounds for several high-dimensional problems including tensor PCA and sparse generalized linear models, as well as novel connections between the sparse rank-one submatrix model and the planted submatrix model.

Our work leaves several open questions, which we briefly outline here. First, our construction of the optimal signed kernel relies on the fact that, under the Gaussian measure, the Hermite polynomials form an orthonormal basis for a suitable function space. Many other distributions also admit orthonormal polynomial bases—for instance, the Laguerre polynomials with respect to the Gamma distribution, and the Gegenbauer polynomials with respect to the Beta distribution; see
Goldstein and Reinert 2005
for more examples. Can our design of the optimal signed kernel be generalized to these source distributions as well? Furthermore, for Laplace, Uniform, and Erlang sources, the optimal signed kernels have been derived using different techniques, such as Fourier inversion; see
Lou et al. 2025
. Can we unify these approaches under a single framework based on orthonormal polynomials?

Second, our technique relies critically on the differentiability of the target density with respect to the location parameter. For instance, when the target is the Laplace location model, the reduction fails due to the density being non-differentiable at a single point. Nevertheless, Gaussian-to-Laplace reductions are of particular interest, for example in differential privacy. Can the optimal Markov kernel defined in Eq. (
9
) overcome this limitation, or does a lower bound—perhaps based on the data processing inequality—rule out such reductions entirely? Furthermore, our reduction requires the target density to satisfy smoothness assumptions such as Assumption
2
and Assumption
3
. While these are met by a broad class of distributions, an important open question is whether these conditions can be relaxed or even removed, or conversely, whether one can establish lower bounds showing that they are indeed necessary.

Finally, we believe the Gaussian-source reduction framework has applications well beyond the settings explored in this paper. For instance, we chose to focus our discussion of universality of computational hardness on tensor PCA. However, the reduction in Theorem
1
is quite general, and could be adapted to regression and denoising models where the observations are corrupted by Gaussian noise. By transferring these to analogous models with non-Gaussian noise satisfying Assumption
4
, one could potentially extend universality results across a broad class of problems, complementing the directions studied in
Brennan et al. 2018
;
Brennan and Bresler 2020
.

5
Proofs of results from Section
2.1

We provide proofs of Proposition
1
and Lemma
1
below. We begin by reviewing some basic properties of the Hermite polynomials defined in Eqs. (
6
) and (
7
), which will be used frequently in our proofs.

First, note that the normalized Hermite polynomials are orthonormal in that
\big\langle\overline{H}_{n}(\cdot),\overline{H}_{m}(\cdot)\big\rangle=\delta_{nm}
, where
\delta_{nm}
is the Kronecker delta with
\delta_{nm}={\mathbb{I}\left\{n=m\right\}}
. The other properties we use are:

\displaystyle\big|H_{n}(x)\exp(-x^{2}/2)\big|\leq\big(\sqrt{\pi}2^{n}n!\big)^{1/2},\qquad\qquad\big|\overline{H}_{n}(x)\exp(-x^{2}/2)\big|\leq 1,

(62a)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}H_{n+1}(x)}{\mathrm{d}x}=2(n+1)H_{n}(x),\qquad\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{H}_{n+1}(x)}{\mathrm{d}x}=\sqrt{2(n+1)}\cdot\overline{H}_{n}(x),

(62b)

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\qquad H_{n}(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor}\frac{n!}{m!(n-2m)!}\cdot(-1)^{m}(2x)^{n-2m}.

(62c)

Inequality (
62a
) can be found in
Szego 1939
, and Eqs. (
62b
) and (
62c
) correspond to
Szego 1939
, respectively.

5.1
Proof of Proposition
1

Recall the signed
\mathcal{S}^{\star}
from Eq. (
13
) and the source density
u(x;\theta)
from Eq. (
4
). It suffices to verify

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{S}^{\star}(y\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x=v(y;\theta)\quad\text{for all}\quad y,\theta\in\real.

(63)

To reduce the notational burden, we let
c_{k}=(-1)^{k}\sigma^{2k}/(2k)!!
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\phi(x)=e^{-x^{2}}/\sqrt{\pi}
for
x\in\real
. In our proof, we separately evaluate the LHS and RHS of Eq. (
63
) in terms of Hermite projections, and then show that these are equal.

LHS of Eq. (
63
).

By applying integration by parts and using Assumption
1
(b), we obtain

\displaystyle\int\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\cdot\phi\bigg(\frac{x-\theta}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\bigg)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\nabla_{x}^{(2k-1)}v(y;x)\cdot\phi\bigg(\frac{x-\theta}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\bigg)\bigg|_{-\infty}^{+\infty}-\int\nabla_{x}^{(2k-1)}v(y;x)\cdot\phi^{\prime}\bigg(\frac{x-\theta}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\bigg)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\int\nabla_{x}^{(2k-1)}v(y;x)\cdot\phi^{\prime}\bigg(\frac{x-\theta}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\bigg)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{=}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\Big)^{2k}\cdot\int v(y;x)\cdot\phi^{(2k)}\bigg(\frac{x-\theta}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\bigg)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{=}\frac{\sqrt{2}\sigma}{2^{k}\sigma^{2k}}\int v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot\phi^{(2k)}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(iii)}}{=}\frac{\sqrt{2}\sigma}{2^{k}\sigma^{2k}}\int v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot H_{2k}(x)\frac{e^{-x^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\mathrm{d}x,

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we perform the integration by parts from the previous step
2k
times. In step
{\sf(ii)}
we use a change of variables, and in step
{\sf(iii)}
we use the definition of Hermite polynomials (
6
).
Consequently, we obtain

\displaystyle\int c_{k}\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int c_{k}\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\phi\bigg(\frac{x-\theta}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\bigg)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{k}}{2^{k}\sigma^{2k}}\int v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot H_{2k}(x)\frac{e^{-x^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\mathrm{d}x.

Now define
f(x;\theta,y)=v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)
for convenience, and recall our definition of the inner product operation
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
from Eq. (
8
). Then

\displaystyle\int c_{k}\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{k}}{\sqrt{\pi}2^{k}\sigma^{2k}}\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),H_{2k}(\cdot)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),\overline{H}_{2k}(\cdot)\big\rangle\cdot\frac{c_{k}}{\sqrt{\pi}2^{k}\sigma^{2k}}\cdot(\sqrt{\pi}2^{2k}(2k)!)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),\overline{H}_{2k}(\cdot)\big\rangle\cdot\overline{H}_{2k}(0).

(64)

To justify Eq. (
5.1
), recall that
c_{k}=(-1)^{k}\sigma^{2k}/(2k)!!
. Then by definition of
\overline{H}_{2k}(x)
in Eq. (
6
), we have

\overline{H}_{2k}(0)=\frac{H_{2k}(0)}{\big(\sqrt{\pi}2^{2k}(2k)!\big)^{1/2}}\overset{{\sf(i)}}{=}\frac{(-1)^{k}(2k)!/k!}{\big(\sqrt{\pi}2^{2k}(2k)!\big)^{1/2}}=\frac{c_{k}}{\sqrt{\pi}2^{k}\sigma^{2k}}\cdot(\sqrt{\pi}2^{2k}(2k)!)^{1/2},

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use
Szego 1939
so that
H_{2k}(0)=(-1)^{k}(2k)!/k!
.

Putting together the pieces, we obtain

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{S}^{\star}(y\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}c_{k}\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{=}\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\int c_{k}\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{=}\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),\overline{H}_{2k}(\cdot)\big\rangle\cdot\overline{H}_{2k}(0),

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use Assumption
1
(d) and Fubini’s theorem so that switching the summation and integral is valid, and in step
{\sf(ii)}
we use Eq. (
5.1
).

RHS of Eq. (
63
).

By Assumption
1
(c),
f(x;\theta,y)
admits a Hermite expansion, that is,

f(x;\theta,y)=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),\overline{H}_{k}(\cdot)\big\rangle\cdot\overline{H}_{k}(x),\quad\text{for all}\quad x,y\in\real.

Setting
x=0
yields for all
y\in\real
,

\displaystyle v(y;\theta)=f(0;\theta,y)=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),\overline{H}_{k}(\cdot)\big\rangle\cdot\overline{H}_{k}(0)=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),\overline{H}_{2k}(\cdot)\big\rangle\cdot\overline{H}_{2k}(0),

(65)

where in the last step we use the fact that
\overline{H}_{k}(0)=0
when
k
is odd; see
Szego 1939
.

Combing the pieces.

Our two steps yield

\int\mathcal{S}^{\star}(y\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x=f(0;\theta,y)=v(y;\theta)\quad\text{for all }y\in\real,\theta\in\Theta.

This concludes the proof. ∎

5.2
Proof of Lemma
1

Before proving the lemma, let us establish the claimed inequality (
16
) for completeness. Applying the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x-v(\cdot;\theta)\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x-\int\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+\Big\|\int\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x-v(\cdot;\theta)\Big\|_{1}.

(66)

By following the same steps as in
Lou et al. 2025
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x-\int\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\Big\|_{1}\leq\int\big(|p(x)-1|+q(x)\big)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x.

(67)

Consequently, substituting Ineq. (
67
) into the RHS of Ineq. (
5.2
) and taking a supremum over
\theta\in\Theta
yields inequality (
16
).

We now turn to proving Ineq. (
18
); the reader is advised to go through the proof of Proposition
1
for context. In that proof, we defined
f(x;\theta,y)=v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)
and
c_{k}=(-1)^{k}\sigma^{2k}/(2k)!!
for convenience. By Assumption
1
(c) and Eq. (
65
), we have

v(y;\theta)=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),\overline{H}_{2k}(\cdot)\big\rangle\cdot\overline{H}_{2k}(0)\quad\text{for all }y\in\real,\theta\in\Theta.

Next,

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int\sum_{k=0}^{N}c_{k}\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\int c_{k}\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{=}\sum_{k=0}^{N}\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),\overline{H}_{2k}(\cdot)\big\rangle\cdot\overline{H}_{2k}(0),

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use Eq. (
5.1
). Combining the two equations above yields

\displaystyle\Big\|\int\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x-v(\cdot;\theta)\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\int\bigg|\int\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x-v(y;\theta)\bigg|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int\bigg|\sum_{k=0}^{N}\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),\overline{H}_{2k}(\cdot)\big\rangle\cdot\overline{H}_{2k}(0)-\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),\overline{H}_{2k}(\cdot)\big\rangle\cdot\overline{H}_{2k}(0)\bigg|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int\bigg|\sum_{k=N+1}^{+\infty}\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),\overline{H}_{2k}(\cdot)\big\rangle\cdot\overline{H}_{2k}(0)\bigg|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}\int\sum_{k=N+1}^{+\infty}\Big|\big\langle f(\cdot;\theta,y),\overline{H}_{2k}(\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int\sum_{k=N+1}^{+\infty}\bigg|\int v(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x)\cdot\overline{H}_{2k}(x)\exp(-x^{2})\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\mathrm{d}y,

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we apply the triangle inequality and use the sequence of bounds

|\overline{H}_{2k}(0)|=\frac{|H_{2k}(0)|}{\big(\sqrt{\pi}2^{2k}(2k)!\big)^{1/2}}=\frac{(2k)!/k!}{\big(\sqrt{\pi}2^{2k}(2k)!\big)^{1/2}}=\frac{(2k-1)!!}{\big(\sqrt{\pi}(2k)!\big)^{1/2}}\leq 1.

Taking supremum over
\theta\in\Theta
on both sides of the inequality in the display above yields the claim. ∎

6
Proofs of results from Section
2.2

We prove Theorem
1
(a) in Section
6.1
and Theorem
1
(b) in Section
6.2
.
We provide the proof of the corollaries and examples following Theorem
1
in Sections
6.3
–
6.5
.

6.1
Proof of Theorem
1
(a)

We will use Lemma
1
to bound
\delta(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star})
by the signed deficiency and other terms, and then proceed to bounding those quantities.
The first part of the proof is dedicated to verifying Assumption
1
for the target density
v(y;\theta)
defined in Eq. (
20
). This will then allow us to apply Lemma
1
.

Assumption
1
(a) immediately holds by Assumption
2
(b). To verify Assumption
1
(b),
note that by definition,
v(y;x)=g(h(x))
where
g(x)=\frac{1}{\tau}\exp(-x)
and
h(x)=\psi((y-x)/\tau)
for all
x,y\in\real
. Applying Faà di Bruno’s formula yields

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{n!}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}\cdot g^{(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n})}(h(x))\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{h^{(j)}(x)}{j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(-h(x))}{\tau}\cdot(n!)\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{(-1)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\psi^{(j)}\bigg(\frac{y-x}{\tau}\bigg)\frac{(-1)^{j}}{\tau^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}},

(68)

where the summation is over all
n
-tuples of nonnegative integers
(m_{1},\cdots,m_{n})
satisfying
\sum_{j=1}^{n}jm_{j}=n
. Note that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the following bound holds and will be used repeatedly:

\displaystyle\bigg|\bigg\{(m_{1},\dots,m_{n}):\sum_{j=1}^{n}jm_{j}=n,m_{j}\geq 0,m_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ for all }j\in[n]\bigg\}\bigg|\leq\frac{n}{1}\cdot\frac{n}{2}\cdots\frac{n}{n}\leq\frac{n^{n}}{n!}\leq e^{n}.

(69)

By applying the triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\exp(-h(x))}{\tau}\cdot(n!)\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big|\psi^{(j)}\big(\frac{y-x}{\tau}\big)\frac{1}{\tau^{j}j!}\big|^{m_{j}}}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\exp(-h(x))}{\tau}\cdot(n!)\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bigg|\psi^{(j)}\bigg(\frac{y-x}{\tau}\bigg)\frac{1}{\tau^{j}j!}\bigg|\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}},

where in the last step we use the multinomial theorem so that

\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big|\psi^{(j)}\big(\frac{y-x}{\tau}\big)\frac{1}{\tau^{j}j!}\big|^{m_{j}}}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}\leq\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bigg|\psi^{(j)}\bigg(\frac{y-x}{\tau}\bigg)\frac{1}{\tau^{j}j!}\bigg|\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}.

Integrating over
x\in\real
and using the change of variables
t=(y-x)/\tau
yields

\displaystyle\int\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)\big|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq n!\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\int\exp(-\psi(t))\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|\psi^{(j)}(t)|}{\tau^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}n!\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}(C_{\psi}n)!\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}n!\cdot e^{n}\cdot(C_{\psi}n)!<+\infty,

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use Assumption
2
(b),
\tau\geq\widetilde{C}_{\psi}
, and
1\leq m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}\leq n
, and in step
{\sf(ii)}
we use the fact that there are at most
e^{n}
terms in the summation; see Ineq. (
69
). Note that
\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)\big|
is a continuous function in
x
by definition (
20
) and Assumption
2
. Using the bounded integral in the display above, we obtain

\lim_{x\uparrow+\infty}\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)\big|=\lim_{x\downarrow-\infty}\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)\big|=0.

This verifies Assumption
1
(b). We next turn to prove Assumption
1
(c). By definition (
20
),

v\big(y;\sqrt{2}\sigma x+\theta\big)=\frac{1}{\tau}\exp\bigg(-\psi\bigg(\frac{y-\sqrt{2}\sigma x-\theta}{\tau}\bigg)\bigg).

Thus,

\displaystyle\int\big(v\big(y;\sqrt{2}\sigma x+\theta\big)\big)^{2}e^{-x^{2}}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int\big(v\big(y;\sqrt{2}\sigma x+\theta\big)\big)^{2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\sigma\tau}\exp\big(-2\psi(t)\big)\mathrm{d}t<+\infty,

where in the second step we use the change of variables
t=\frac{y-\sqrt{2}\sigma x-\theta}{\tau}
, and in the last step we use Assumption
2
(a). This proves Assumption
1
(c).

Having verified Assumption
2
, we can now apply Lemma
1
to obtain

\displaystyle\delta\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{T}^{\star}_{N}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\varsigma\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\theta\in\real}\;\int\big(|p(x)-1|+q(x)\big)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x.

(70)

To bound the terms on the RHS of the above display, we use the following lemma.

Lemma 3
.

Let the source and target statistical models
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
be given by Eq. (
2
) and Eq. (
20
), respectively. Let the signed kernel
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}
be defined in Eq. (
14
) and let
\varsigma\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}\big)
be defined in Eq. (
10
). Suppose the tuple of parameters
(N,\tau,\lambda,\sigma)
satisfies condition (
27
) and Assumption
2
holds. Let
\overline{C}_{\psi}
be defined in Eq. (
23
). Then the following two parts hold.

(a)

We have

\displaystyle\varsigma\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}\big)\leq\overline{C}_{\psi}\exp\bigg(-(2e)^{-1}(2N+1)^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\bigg),

(71)

(b)

Let
p(x)
and
q(x)
be defined in Eq. (
17
). Let
\mathsf{Err}(\psi,n,\lambda)
be defined in Eq. (
25
). Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\theta\in\real}\;\int\big(|p(x)-1|+q(x)\big)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\leq 4\mathsf{Err}(\psi,2N,\lambda)

(72)

Indeed, substituting Ineq. (
71
) and Ineq. (
72
) into the RHS of Ineq. (
70
) proves Theorem
1
(a). ∎

It remains to prove Lemma
3
; we do so in the following subsections.

6.1.1
Proof of Lemma
3
(a)

By Lemma
1
(Ineq. (
18
)), we have

\displaystyle\varsigma\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}\big)\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\theta\in\real}\;\int\sum_{k=N+1}^{+\infty}\bigg|\int v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot\overline{H}_{2k}(x)e^{-x^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\mathrm{d}y.

(73)

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
a_{n}=\sqrt{2(n+1)}
for convenience.
En route to bounding the RHS of the inequality in the display above, let us apply integration by parts and use Eq. (
62b
). We obtain that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\int v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot\overline{H}_{n}(x)e^{-x^{2}}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a_{n}}\int v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot e^{-x^{2}}\mathrm{d}\overline{H}_{n+1}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a_{n}}\Big[v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)e^{-x^{2}}\overline{H}_{n+1}(x)\Big]\Big|_{-\infty}^{+\infty}-\frac{1}{a_{n}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\big[v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot e^{-x^{2}}\big]}{\mathrm{d}x}\overline{H}_{n+1}(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Performing the same integration by parts
r
times yields

\displaystyle\int v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot\overline{H}_{n}(x)e^{-x^{2}}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{r-1}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\prod_{j=0}^{\ell}a_{n+j}}\bigg[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\ell}\big[v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)e^{-x^{2}}\big]}{\mathrm{d}x^{\ell}}\overline{H}_{n+1+\ell}(x)\bigg]\bigg|_{-\infty}^{+\infty}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(-1)^{r}}{\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}a_{n+j}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}\big[v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot e^{-x^{2}}\big]}{\mathrm{d}x^{r}}\overline{H}_{n+r}(x)\mathrm{d}x.

(74)

We claim the following equation holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
y\in\real
, and
\tau\geq\sqrt{2}\sigma\widetilde{C}_{\psi},\sigma>0
:

\displaystyle\bigg[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\ell}\big[v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)e^{-x^{2}}\big]}{\mathrm{d}x^{\ell}}\overline{H}_{n+1+\ell}(x)\bigg]\bigg|_{-\infty}^{+\infty}=0,

(75)

where the evaluation at infinity is with respect to variable
x
. We defer the proof of claim (
75
) to the end of this section.
Applying the product rule for higher-order derivative yields for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}\big[v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot e^{-x^{2}}\big]}{\mathrm{d}x^{r}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{r}\binom{r}{k}\nabla_{x}^{(r-k)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}e^{-x^{2}}}{\mathrm{d}x^{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{r}\binom{r}{k}\nabla_{x}^{(r-k)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot(-1)^{k}H_{k}(x)e^{-x^{2}}.

(76)

Substituting Eq. (
6.1.1
) and Eq. (
75
) into Eq. (
6.1.1
) and applying the triangle inequality yields

\displaystyle\int\bigg|\int v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot\overline{H}_{n}(x)e^{-x^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}a_{n+j}}\sum_{k=0}^{r}\binom{r}{k}\int\int\bigg|\nabla_{x}^{(r-k)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot H_{k}(x)e^{-x^{2}}\cdot\overline{H}_{n+r}(x)\bigg|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}a_{n+j}}\sum_{k=0}^{r}\binom{r}{k}\cdot\sqrt{\gamma_{k}}\int\int\bigg|\nabla_{x}^{(r-k)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot e^{-x^{2}/2}\bigg|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,

(77)

where in the last step we let
\gamma_{k}=\sqrt{\pi}2^{k}k!
and use
\big|H_{k}(x)e^{-x^{2}/2}\big|\leq\sqrt{\gamma_{k}}
and also
\big|\overline{H}_{n+r}(x)\big|\leq 1
for all
x\in\real
, by Ineq. (
62a
). We further claim that for all
r,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
k\leq r
, and for all
\tau\geq 4\sqrt{2}e\sigma\widetilde{C}_{\psi}
, we have

\displaystyle\int\int\bigg|\nabla_{x}^{(r-k)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot e^{-x^{2}/2}\bigg|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\leq 4\pi(r-k)!\cdot\frac{1}{4^{r-k}}\cdot\big(C_{\psi}(r-k)\big)!

(78)

We defer the proof of Claim (
78
) to the end of this section.
Substituting Ineq. (
78
) into Ineq. (
6.1.1
) yields

\displaystyle\int\bigg|\int v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot\overline{H}_{n}(x)e^{-x^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\pi}{\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}a_{n+j}}\sum_{k=0}^{r}\binom{r}{k}\cdot\sqrt{\gamma_{k}}\cdot\frac{(r-k)!}{4^{r-k}}\cdot\big(C_{\psi}(r-k)\big)!

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}\frac{4\pi^{5/4}\cdot r!\cdot\big(C_{\psi}\cdot r\big)!\cdot 2^{r/2}}{\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}a_{n+j}}\sum_{k=0}^{r}\frac{1}{4^{r-k}}

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}\frac{8\pi^{5/4}\cdot r!\cdot\big(C_{\psi}\cdot r\big)!\cdot 2^{r/2}}{2^{r/2}\cdot n^{r/2}}=\frac{8\pi^{5/4}\cdot r!\cdot\big(C_{\psi}\cdot r\big)!}{n^{r/2}},

(79)

where in the step
{\sf(i)}
we use
\binom{r}{k}=r!/\big((r-k)!\cdot k!\big)
and
\gamma_{k}=\sqrt{\pi}2^{k}\cdot k!
, and in step
{\sf(ii)}
we recall that
a_{n+j}=\sqrt{2(n+j+1)}\geq\sqrt{2n}
for all
j
and
\sum_{k=0}^{r}4^{-(r-k)}\leq 2
. Note that the inequality in the display above holds for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. We now set

r=\left\lfloor n^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}/\big(e\big(C_{\psi}\vee 1\big)\big)\right\rfloor\quad\text{so that}\quad\frac{\big(\big(C_{\psi}\vee 1\big)r\big)^{1+C_{\psi}}}{\sqrt{n}}\leq e^{-(1+C_{\psi})}.

Consequently, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{r!\cdot\big(C_{\psi}\cdot r\big)!}{n^{r/2}}\leq\frac{r^{r}\cdot\big(\big(C_{\psi}\vee 1\big)r\big)^{C_{\psi}r}}{n^{r/2}}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{\big(\big(C_{\psi}\vee 1\big)r\big)^{1+C_{\psi}}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)^{r}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-(1+C_{\psi})r}\leq e^{1+C_{\psi}}\exp\Big(-e^{-1}n^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\Big),

(80)

where in the last step we use that by definition, we have
r\geq\frac{n^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}}{e(C_{\psi}\vee 1)}-1
. Substituting Ineq. (
6.1.1
) into the RHS of Ineq. (
6.1.1
) yields that for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle\int\bigg|\int v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot\overline{H}_{n}(x)e^{-x^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\mathrm{d}y\leq 8\pi^{5/4}e^{1+C_{\psi}}\exp\Big(-e^{-1}n^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\Big).

Substituting the inequality in the display above into the RHS of Ineq. (
73
) yields

\displaystyle\varsigma\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 4\pi^{5/4}e^{1+C_{\psi}}\sum_{n=2N+2}^{+\infty}\exp\Big(-e^{-1}n^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 4\pi^{5/4}e^{1+C_{\psi}}\int_{2N+1}^{+\infty}\exp\Big(-e^{-1}t^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\Big)\mathrm{d}t.

To conclude, we must bound the integral in the display above. To reduce the notational burden, let
a=2N+1
,
b=2(1+C_{\psi})
, and
C=e^{e^{-1}}>1
. Note that
\frac{ba^{-1/b}}{\log(C)}\leq 1
by assumption. We obtain

\displaystyle\int_{2N+1}^{+\infty}\exp\Big(-e^{-1}t^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\Big)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{+\infty}C^{-t^{1/b}}\mathrm{d}t=b\int_{a^{1/b}}^{+\infty}z^{b-1}e^{-\log(C)z}\mathrm{d}z,

where in the last step we use the change of variables
z=t^{1/b}
. Integrating by parts then yields

\displaystyle\int_{a^{1/b}}^{+\infty}z^{b-1}e^{-\log(C)z}\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=e^{-\log(C)a^{1/b}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{a^{\frac{b-1}{b}}}{\log(C)}+\frac{(b-1)a^{\frac{b-2}{b}}}{\log(C)^{2}}+\frac{(b-1)(b-2)a^{\frac{b-3}{b}}}{\log(C)^{3}}+\cdots+\frac{b!}{\log(C)^{b}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\log(C)a^{1/b}}\cdot\frac{a^{\frac{b-1}{b}}b}{\log(C)}.

Here the second step uses the fact that since
\frac{ba^{-1/b}}{\log(C)}\leq 1
, the series is decreasing, and thus the total sum can be bounded by the first term multiplied by the total number of terms. Putting all the pieces together, we have obtained for
2N+1\geq(2e(1+C_{\psi}))^{2(1+C_{\psi})}
,

\displaystyle\varsigma\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 16\pi^{\frac{5}{4}}e^{2+C_{\psi}}(1+C_{\psi})^{2}(2N+1)\exp\bigg(-e^{-1}(2N+1)^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=16\pi^{\frac{5}{4}}e^{2+C_{\psi}}(1+C_{\psi})^{2}\exp\bigg(-(2e)^{-1}(2N+1)^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad\times(2e)^{2(1+C_{\psi})}\cdot\frac{2N+1}{(2e)^{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\cdot\exp\bigg(-(2e)^{-1}(2N+1)^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}16\pi^{\frac{5}{4}}e^{2+C_{\psi}}(1+C_{\psi})^{2}(2e)^{2(1+C_{\psi})}\exp\bigg(-(2e)^{-1}(2N+1)^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\bigg),

where in step
{\sf(i)}
use the numerical inequality

\displaystyle\frac{t}{(2e)^{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\leq\exp\Big((2e)^{-1}t^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\Big)\quad\text{for all }t\geq(2e)^{2(1+C_{\psi})}e^{4(1+C_{\psi})^{2}}.

(81)

In particular, we apply this inequality for
t=2N+1
, which is in the desired range by assumption. To conclude, note that Ineq. (
71
) holds by recalling the definition
\overline{C}_{\psi}
in Eq. (
23
).

It remains to verify the claims (
75
), (
78
), and (
81
).

Proof of Claim (
75
).

By definition (
20
), we obtain
v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)=g(h(x))
, where
g(x)=\exp(-x)/\tau
and
h(x)=\psi\big(\big(y-\theta-\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)/\tau\big)
.
Applying the Faà di Bruno’s formula yields

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{n!}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}\cdot g^{(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n})}(h(x))\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{h^{(j)}(x)}{j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(-h(x))}{\tau}\cdot(n!)\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{(-1)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\psi^{(j)}\big(\frac{y-\theta-\sqrt{2}\sigma x}{\tau}\big)\cdot\big(-\sqrt{2}\sigma\big)^{j}}{\tau^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}},

(82)

where the summation is over all
n
-tuples of nonnegative integers
(m_{1},\cdots,m_{n})
satisfying
\sum_{j=1}^{n}jm_{j}=n
. Applying the triangle inequality and using the multinomial theorem yield

\displaystyle\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\exp(-h(x))}{\tau}\cdot(n!)\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\big|\psi^{(j)}\big(\frac{y-\theta-\sqrt{2}\sigma x}{\tau}\big)\big|\cdot\big(\sqrt{2}\sigma\big)^{j}}{\tau^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\exp(-h(x))}{\tau}\cdot(n!)\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\big|\psi^{(j)}\big(\frac{y-\theta-\sqrt{2}\sigma x}{\tau}\big)\big|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}},

where in the last step we use
\tau\geq\sqrt{2}\sigma\widetilde{C}_{\psi}
. Integrating over
x\in\real
and using change of variables
t=\frac{y-\theta-\sqrt{2}\sigma x}{\tau}
yield that for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
y\in\real
,

\displaystyle\int\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\big|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n!}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\int\exp(-\psi(t))\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|\psi^{(j)}(t)|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n!}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\cdot e^{n}\cdot(C_{\psi}n)!<+\infty,

where we have used Assumption
2
and the fact that there are at most
e^{n}
terms in the summation. Combining the inequality in the display with Eq. (
6.1.1
) and applying the triangle inequality yield

\displaystyle\int\bigg|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\ell}\big[v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot e^{-x^{2}}\big]}{\mathrm{d}x^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{H}_{n+1+\ell}(x)\bigg|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{k}\int\big|\nabla_{x}^{(\ell-k)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\big|\cdot\big|\overline{H}_{n+1+\ell}(x)\cdot H_{k}(x)e^{-x^{2}}\big|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{k}\big(\sqrt{\pi}2^{k}k!\big)^{1/2}\int\big|\nabla_{x}^{(\ell-k)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\big|\mathrm{d}x<+\infty

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use the boundness property of Hermite polynomial (
62a
). Consequently, for a positive and continuous function, if the integral over is finite, then this function must converge to zero at infinity. Thus,

\displaystyle\lim_{|x|\uparrow+\infty}\;\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\ell}\big[v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\cdot e^{-x^{2}}\big]}{\mathrm{d}x^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{H}_{n+1+\ell}(x)=0.

This proves Claim (
75
).

Proof of Claim (
78
).

Using Eq. (
6.1.1
) with the triangle inequality yields for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\exp(-h(x))}{\tau}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{n!}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\big|\psi^{(j)}\big(\frac{y-\theta-\sqrt{2}\sigma x}{\tau}\big)\big|\cdot\big(\sqrt{2}\sigma\big)^{j}}{\tau^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2}\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\sigma}{\tau}\bigg)^{n}\cdot\frac{\exp(-h(x))}{\tau}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{n!}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\big|\psi^{(j)}\big(\frac{y-\theta-\sqrt{2}\sigma x}{\tau}\big)\big|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}},

where in the second step we use
\sum_{j=1}^{n}jm_{j}=n
so that

\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2}\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\sigma}{\tau}\bigg)^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{1}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\big)^{j}}\bigg)^{m_{j}}=\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2}\sigma}{\tau}\bigg)^{n}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\big(\sqrt{2}\sigma\big)^{j}}{\tau^{j}}\bigg)^{m_{j}}.

Continuing and applying the multinomial theorem yields

\displaystyle\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\big|\leq\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2}\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\sigma}{\tau}\bigg)^{n}\cdot(n!)\cdot\frac{\exp(-h(x))}{\tau}\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\big|\psi^{(j)}\big(\frac{y-\theta-\sqrt{2}\sigma x}{\tau}\big)\big|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}.

Recall that we let
h(x)=\psi\big(\big(y-\theta-\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)/\tau\big)
. Integrating the above expression over
x,y\in\real
then yields

\displaystyle\int\int\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\big|e^{-x^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2}\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\sigma}{\tau}\bigg)^{n}\cdot(n!)\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\int\int\frac{1}{\tau}e^{-\psi\big(\frac{y-\theta-\sqrt{2}\sigma x}{\tau}\big)}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\big|\psi^{(j)}\big(\frac{y-\theta-\sqrt{2}\sigma x}{\tau}\big)\big|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}\mathrm{d}y\cdot e^{-x^{2}/2}\mathrm{dx}

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{=}\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2}\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\sigma}{\tau}\bigg)^{n}\cdot(n!)\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\int\int\exp(-\psi(t))\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\big|\psi^{(j)}(t)\big|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}\mathrm{d}t\cdot e^{-x^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2}\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\sigma}{\tau}\bigg)^{n}\cdot(n!)\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}(C_{\psi}n)!\cdot\int e^{-x^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2}\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\sigma}{\tau}\bigg)^{n}\cdot(n!)\cdot e^{n}\cdot(C_{\psi}n)!\cdot\sqrt{2\pi},

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we first integrate over
y\in\real
and use the change of variables
t=(y-\theta-\sqrt{2}\sigma x)/\tau
, in step
{\sf(ii)}
we use Assumption
2
, and in the last step we use there are at most
e^{n}
terms in the summation (see Ineq. (
69
)) and also that
\int e^{-x^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{2\pi}
. Thus, by letting
\tau\geq 4\sqrt{2}e\sigma\widetilde{C}_{\psi}
, we obtain

\int\int\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}\sigma x\big)\big|e^{-x^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\leq\sqrt{2\pi}(n!)\cdot(C_{\psi}n)!\cdot\frac{1}{4^{n}}\quad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

This proves Claim (
78
).

Proof of Claim (
81
).

By letting
x=(2e)^{-1}t^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{\psi})}}
, it is equivalent to show

x^{2(1+C_{\psi})}\leq e^{x}\quad\text{for all }x\geq e^{2(1+C_{\psi})},

which is equivalent to, by taking the log on both sides and re-arranging the terms,

h(x):=2(1+C_{\psi})\frac{\log(x)}{x}\leq 1\quad\text{for all }x\geq e^{2(1+C_{\psi})}.

To show this, simply note that the function
h(x)
is monotone decreasing for all
x\geq 1
. Thus,

h(x)\leq h\big(e^{2(1+C_{\psi})}\big)=\frac{(2(1+C_{\psi}))^{2}}{e^{2(1+C_{\psi})}}\leq\sup_{a>0}\frac{a^{2}}{e^{a}}=\frac{2^{2}}{e^{2}}\leq 1\quad\text{for all }x\geq e^{2(1+C_{\psi})},

as claimed.
∎

6.1.2
Proof of Lemma
3
(b)

We first state an auxiliary lemma and defer its proof to Appendix
B.2
.

Lemma 4
.

Consider the signed kernel
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)
defined in Eq. (
14
) for the target density
v(y;\theta)
defined in Eq. (
20
). Let the set
\mathcal{A}(\psi,n,\lambda)
, its complement
\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,n,\lambda)
, and the scalar
\mathsf{Err}(\psi,n,\lambda)
be defined as in Eq. (
24
) and Eq. (
25
), respectively. Suppose Assumption
2
holds with the pair of constants
(C_{\psi},\widetilde{C}_{\psi})
. Then the following holds for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lambda>0
.

(i)

If
\tau\geq 8e\lambda\sigma N
, then
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\geq 0
as long as
(y-x)/\tau\in\mathcal{A}(\psi,2N,\lambda)
.

(ii)

Let
\rho(t)=\exp(-\psi(t))
for all
t\in\real
. Then for all
1\leq n\leq 2N
and
x\in\real
, we have

\displaystyle\int\rho^{(n)}(t)\mathrm{d}t=0\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\frac{y-x}{\tau}\in\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,2N,\lambda)}\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)\big|\mathrm{d}y\leq\frac{n!\cdot\lambda^{n}\cdot e^{n}}{\tau^{n}}\cdot\mathsf{Err}(\psi,2N,\lambda).

(83)

We now use the lemma above to prove inequality (
72
). Note that
v(y;x)=\frac{1}{\tau}\rho(\frac{y-x}{\tau})
. Consequently, we obtain for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
x,y\in\real
that

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)=\frac{1}{\tau}\rho^{(n)}\bigg(\frac{y-x}{\tau}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{-1}{\tau}\bigg)^{n}.

(84)

Let us now upper bound
p(x)
(
17
). Using part (i) of Lemma
4
and definition (
17
), we obtain

\displaystyle p(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\frac{y-x}{\tau}\in\mathcal{A}(\psi,2N,\lambda)}\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\mathrm{d}y+\int_{\frac{y-x}{\tau}\in\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,2N,\lambda)}\big|\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\big|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\int\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\mathrm{d}y+2\int_{\frac{y-x}{\tau}\in\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,2N,\lambda)}\big|\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\big|\mathrm{d}y.

(85)

Next, by definition, we have

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\mathrm{d}y=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k}\sigma^{2k}}{(2k)!!}\int\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y\overset{{\sf(i)}}{=}\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k}\sigma^{2k}}{(2k)!!}\int\frac{\rho^{(2k)}(t)}{\tau^{2k}}\mathrm{d}t=1,

(86)

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use Eq. (
84
) and use the change of variables
t=(y-x)/\tau
, and in the last step we use Ineq. (
83
) and note that
\int\rho(t)\mathrm{d}t=1
. Towards bounding the second term on the RHS of Ineq. (
6.1.2
), we obtain by using the triangle inequality that

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{y-x}{\tau}\in\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,2N,\lambda)}\big|\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\big|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{\sigma^{2k}}{(2k)!!}\int_{\frac{y-x}{\tau}\in\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,2N,\lambda)}\big|\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\big|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}\int_{\frac{y-x}{\tau}\in\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,2N,\lambda)}v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma^{2k}}{(2k)!!}\frac{(2k)!\cdot\lambda^{2k}\cdot e^{2k}}{\tau^{2k}}\cdot\mathsf{Err}(\psi,2N,\lambda)

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}2\mathsf{Err}(\psi,2N,\lambda),

(87)

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use Ineq. (
83
). In step
{\sf(ii)}
we use

\int_{\frac{y-x}{\tau}\in\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,2N,\lambda)}v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\frac{y-x}{\tau}\in\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,2N,\lambda)}\frac{\rho(\frac{y-x}{\tau})}{\tau}\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,2N,\lambda)}e^{-\psi(t)}\mathrm{d}t\leq\mathsf{Err}(\psi,2N,\lambda),

and also use
\tau\geq 8e\lambda\sigma N
so that we have the chain of bounds

\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma^{2k}\cdot(2k)!\cdot(e\lambda)^{2k}}{(2k)!!\cdot\tau^{2k}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{N}\bigg(\frac{2e\lambda\sigma k}{\tau}\bigg)^{2k}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{4^{k}}\leq 1.

Substituting Eq. (
86
) and Ineq. (
6.1.2
) into the RHS of Ineq. (
6.1.2
) yields

\displaystyle p(x)\leq 1+4\mathsf{Err}(\psi,2N,\lambda)\quad\text{for all }x\in\real.

We now bound
q(x)
(
17
). We obtain by definition that

p(x)-q(x)=\int\big[\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\vee 0\big]\mathrm{d}y+\int\big[\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\wedge 0\big]\mathrm{d}y=\int\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\mathrm{d}y=1,

where in the last step we use Eq. (
86
). Note that
q(x)\geq 0
by its definition (
17
) and thus,

\displaystyle p(x)=q(x)+1\geq 1\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in\real.

(88)

Putting the pieces together yields

\big|p(x)-1\big|+q(x)\leq 2\big|p(x)-1\big|\leq 8\mathsf{Err}(\psi,2N,\lambda)\quad\text{for all }x\in\real.

Note that the RHS of the inequality in the display is independent of
x
. Integrating it over the density
u(x;\theta)
and taking a supremum over
\theta
, we complete the proof of Ineq. (
72
).
∎

6.2
Proof of Theorem
1
(b)

We split the proof into several parts.

Constructing the reduction based on a general rejection kernel.

We define a class of base measures
\left\{\mathcal{D}(\cdot\mid x)\right\}_{x\in\mathbb{R}}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(y\mid x)=\frac{1}{2\tau}\exp\bigg(-\psi\bigg(\frac{y-x}{2\tau}\bigg)\bigg),\quad\text{for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}.

(89)

We recall the general rejection kernel
x\mapsto\textsc{rk}(x,T,M,y_{0})
from
Lou et al. 2025
, and employ it using the base measures
\mathcal{D}(y\mid x)
defined in (
89
) and the signed kernel
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)
defined in (
14
). The rejection kernel
rk
requires as input a source sample
x
, the total number of iterations
T
, and a scalar
M>0
that is required to satisfy, for input
x
, the bound

\frac{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\vee 0}{\mathcal{D}(y\mid x)}\leq M,\quad\text{for all }y\in\mathbb{R}.

An output sample point
y_{0}
is also required, and serves as an initialization for the algorithm.

Staying faithful to the theorem statement, we consider
T_{\mathsf{iter}}
to be any nonnegative integer, set
y_{0}=0
, and recall
M(\psi,2N,\lambda)
in Eq. (
26
). We will now construct our reduction algorithm from the rejection kernel and show that it satisfies the conditions required for
rk
to succeed. For
X_{\theta}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta,\sigma^{2})
, define

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta}):=\textsc{rk}\big(X_{\theta},T_{\mathsf{iter}},4M(\psi,2N,\lambda),y_{0}\big).

(90)

Note that for all
x,y\in\real
, we have by definition of
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)
(
14
),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\vee 0}{\mathcal{D}(y\mid x)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{\sigma^{2k}}{(2k)!!}\frac{\big|\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\big|}{\mathcal{D}(y\mid x)}

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}2e^{-\psi(\frac{y-x}{\tau})+\psi(\frac{y-x}{2\tau})}\Bigg(1+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma^{2k}(2k)!\lambda^{2k}}{(2k)!!\tau^{2k}}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{2k}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\frac{|\psi^{(j)}(\frac{y-x}{\tau})|}{\lambda^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{2k}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}2M(\psi,2N,\lambda)+2\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma^{2k}(2k)^{2k}\lambda^{2k}}{\tau^{2k}}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{2k}}M(\psi,2N,\lambda)\leq 4M(\psi,2N,\lambda),

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use Ineq. (
B.2
) and Eq. (
89
), in step
{\sf(ii)}
we use definition (
26
) so that

\displaystyle e^{-\psi(\frac{y-x}{\tau})+\psi(\frac{y-x}{2\tau})}\leq M(\psi,2N,\lambda),\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle e^{-\psi(\frac{y-x}{\tau})+\psi(\frac{y-x}{2\tau})}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\frac{|\psi^{(j)}(\frac{y-x}{\tau})|}{\lambda^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{2k}}\leq M(\psi,2k,\lambda)\leq M(\psi,2N,\lambda).

In the last step, we use the fact that
\tau\geq 8e\sigma\lambda N
, and also that there are at most
e^{2k}
many
2k
-tuples of nonnegative integers
(m_{1},\cdots,m_{2k})
satisfying
\sum_{j=1}^{2k}jm_{j}=2k
. Therefore, the reduction
\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})
in Eq. (
90
) satisfies the conditions required by
rk
.

Computational complexity of the reduction.

Note that from
Lou et al. 2025
, the reduction procedure defined in (
90
) requires, at each iteration, generating a sample form
\mathsf{Unif}([0,1])
and a sample from base measures (
89
), which takes time
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathsf{samp}})
by Assumption
2
(c). It also requires evaluating
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}
once per iteration, which takes time
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N))
by Assumption
1
. Moreover, the total number of iterations is
T_{\mathsf{iter}}
. Consequently, the reduction
\mathsf{K}
(
90
) has computational complexity

\mathcal{O}\Big(T_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot T_{\mathsf{samp}}\cdot T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N)\Big).

Proof of TV deficiency guarantee of the reduction.

We now turn to prove Eq. (
30
). Applying the triangle inequality as in Ineq. (
125
) yields
