\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\real}\;\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta}),Y_{\theta}\big)\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\theta\in\real}\bigg\|\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})-\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\bigg\|_{1}+\delta\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}\big).

(91)

We next turn to bound the two terms in the RHS of the inequality in the display. Applying Ineq. (
28
) and using
2N+1\geq\big(2e\log\big(3\overline{C}_{\psi}/\epsilon\big)\big)^{2(1+C_{\psi})}
yield

\displaystyle\delta\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}\big)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{3}+4\mathsf{Err}(\psi,2N,\lambda).

(92)

Towards bounding the first term of the RHS of Ineq. (
91
), we let
Y=\textsc{rk}\big(x,T_{\mathsf{iter}},4M(\psi,2N,\lambda),y_{0}\big)
for each
x\in\real
.
Then from
Lou et al. 2025
, we obtain that the random variable
Y
is drawn from the mixture distribution

\displaystyle f_{Y}(y\mid x)=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\vee 0}{p(x)}\cdot\big(1-\ell(x)\big)+\delta_{y_{0}}(y)\cdot\ell(x),\quad\text{for all }y\in\real,

(93)

where
p(x)
is defined in Eq. (
17
),
\delta_{y_{0}}(y)
is the Dirac delta function centered at
y_{0}
, and

\displaystyle\ell(x)=\bigg(1-\frac{p(x)}{4M(\psi,2N,\lambda)}\bigg)^{T_{\mathsf{iter}}}.

(94)

By the law of total probability and definition of
\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})
in Eq. (
90
), we obtain that the marginal distribution of
\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})
is given by

\displaystyle f_{\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})}(y)

\displaystyle=\int f_{Y}(y\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int\bigg(\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\big(1-\ell(x)\big)+\delta_{y_{0}}(y)\cdot\ell(x)\bigg)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x,

(95)

where we use Eq. (
93
) and also note by definition (
15
),
\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\vee 0}{p(x)}
. Continuing, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg\|\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})-\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\bigg\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\int\bigg|f_{\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})}(y)-\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{=}\int\bigg|\int\Big(-\ell(x)\cdot\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)+\delta_{y_{0}}(y)\cdot\ell(x)\Big)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}2\int|\ell(x)|u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(iii)}}{\leq}2\exp\bigg(-\frac{T_{\mathsf{iter}}}{4M(\psi,2N,\lambda)}\bigg),

(96)

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use Eq. (
6.2
), and in step
{\sf(ii)}
we apply triangle inequality and use

\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\mathrm{d}y=1\quad\text{and}\quad\int\delta_{y_{0}}(y)\mathrm{d}y=1.

In step
{\sf(iii)}
, we use Ineq. (
88
):
p(x)\geq 1
for all
x\in\real
, so that from Eq. (
94
),

\ell(x)\leq\exp\bigg(-\frac{T_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot p(x)}{4M(\psi,2N,\lambda)}\bigg)\leq\exp\bigg(-\frac{T_{\mathsf{iter}}}{4M(\psi,2N,\lambda)}\bigg)

Substituting Ineq. (
6.2
) and Ineq. (
92
) into the RHS of Ineq. (
91
) completes the proof of guarantee (
30
).
∎

6.3
Proof of Corollary
1

Note that since
\psi\in\mathcal{F}(R)
(
2
), we immediately obtain that Assumption
2
(b) holds with
\widetilde{C}_{\psi}=R
and
C_{\psi}=0
. Similarly, we obtain
\mathcal{A}(\psi,n,R)=\real
and
\mathcal{A}(\psi,n,R)=\emptyset
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Consequently, we have

\mathsf{Err}(\psi,n,R)=0\quad\text{and}\quad M(\psi,n,R)\leq 2\sup_{t\in\real}\Big\{e^{-\psi(t)+\psi(t/2)}\Big\}=\mathcal{O}(1)\quad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

Applying Ineq. (
30
), by setting
T_{\mathsf{iter}}=\mathcal{O}\big(\log(3/\epsilon)\big)
, we obtain
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta}),Y_{\theta}\big)\leq\epsilon
. Note that eventually we take

\lambda=R,\quad N=\mathcal{O}\big(\log^{2}(C/\epsilon)\big),\quad\text{and}\quad\tau=\mathcal{O}\big(\sigma R\log^{2}(C/\epsilon)\big),

so that conditions (
27
) and (
29
) are satisfied.

Finally, we bound the computational complexity of the reduction. Note that we can use Eq. (
6.1
) to evaluate the signed kernel
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}
(
14
). There are at most
\mathcal{O}\big(e^{\pi\sqrt{n}}\big)
many terms in the summation of Eq. (
6.1
) and each term in the summation can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}\big(e^{C\sqrt{n}}\big)
. Also, evaluating
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}
(
14
) only requires computing
\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)
for
1\leq n\leq\mathcal{O}\big(\log^{2}(C/\epsilon)\big)
. Thus, we obtain

T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N)=\mathcal{O}\Big(e^{C^{\prime}\sqrt{N}}\Big)=\mathsf{poly}(1/\epsilon).

Combining with
T_{\mathsf{iter}}=\mathcal{O}\big(\log(3/\epsilon)\big)
yields that the overall computational complexity is of the order
T_{\mathsf{samp}}\cdot\mathsf{poly}(1/\epsilon)
.
∎

6.4
Proof of claims in Example
1

We provide proofs for the different families of distributions separately.

Student’s t-distribution.

Note that
\psi(x)=\frac{\nu+1}{2}h(g(x))
for
h(x)=\log(x)
and
g(x)=1+x^{2}/\nu
. Applying Faà di Bruno’s formula yields

\displaystyle\psi^{(n)}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu+1}{2}\sum_{m_{1}+2m_{2}=n}\frac{n!}{m_{1}!m_{2}!}h^{(m_{1}+m_{2})}\big(g(x)\big)\prod_{j=1}^{2}\bigg(\frac{g^{(j)}(x)}{j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu+1}{2}\cdot n!\cdot\sum_{m_{1}+2m_{2}=n}\frac{(-1)^{m_{1}+m_{2}-1}(m_{1}+m_{2}-1)!}{m_{1}!m_{2}!}\frac{(2x/\nu)^{m_{1}}(1/\nu)^{m_{2}}}{(1+x^{2}/\nu)^{m_{1}+m_{2}-1}},

where the summation is over all nonnegative integers
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
such that
m_{1}+2m_{2}=n
. Using the above closed form equation, we can evaluate
\psi^{(n)}(x)
in time
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathsf{poly}(n)\big)
. Applying the triangle inequality yields

\displaystyle\big|\psi^{(n)}(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu+1}{2}\cdot n!\cdot\sum_{m_{1}+2m_{2}=n}\frac{(m_{1}+m_{2})!}{m_{1}!m_{2}!}\frac{(2|x|/\nu)^{m_{1}}(1/\nu)^{m_{2}}}{(1+x^{2}/\nu)^{m_{1}+m_{2}}}

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}\frac{\nu+1}{2}\cdot n!\cdot\sum_{m_{1}+2m_{2}=n}\bigg(\frac{\frac{2|x|+1}{\nu}}{1+x^{2}/\nu}\bigg)^{m_{1}+m_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu+1}{2}\cdot n!\cdot(3e)^{n},

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use the multinomial theorem. In step
{\sf(ii)}
we use

\frac{\frac{2|x|+1}{\nu}}{1+x^{2}/\nu}\leq\frac{x^{2}+2}{\nu+x^{2}}\leq 1+\frac{2}{\nu}\leq 3,

the fact that
m_{1}+m_{2}\leq n
and that there are at most
e^{n}
terms in the summation. Consequently, by letting
R=6e(\nu+1)
, we obtain

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|\psi^{(j)}(x)|}{R^{j}j!}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2^{j}}\leq 1\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in\real,n\in\mathbb{N}.

Thus,
\psi\in\mathcal{F}(6e(\nu+1))
. Note that for all
t\in\real
, we have

\psi(t)-\psi(t/2)=\frac{\nu+1}{2}\log\bigg(\frac{1+t^{2}/v}{1+t^{2}/4v}\bigg)\geq\frac{1}{2}\log(5/4).

Consequently,
\sup_{t\in\real}\big\{e^{-\psi(t)+\psi(t/2)}\big\}\leq\sqrt{4/5}\leq 1
. This completes the proof for Student’s t-distribution.
\clubsuit

Hyperbolic secant distribution.

By letting
h(t)=\log(t)
and
g(t)=e^{\frac{\pi t}{2}}+e^{-\frac{\pi t}{2}}
, we have
\psi(t)=h(g(t))
. Now, applying Faà di Bruno’s formula yields

\displaystyle\big|\psi^{(n)}(t)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{n!}{(m_{1}!)\cdots(m_{n}!)}\cdot\big|h^{(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n})}(g(t))\big|\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{\big|g^{(j)}(t)\big|}{j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq n!\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n})!}{(m_{1}!)\cdots(m_{n}!)}\frac{1}{(g(t))^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{(\frac{\pi}{2})^{j}g(t)}{j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq n!\cdot(\pi/2)^{n}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n})!}{(m_{1}!)\cdots(m_{n}!)}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{1}{j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq n!\cdot(\pi/2)^{n}\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{j!}\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}\leq n!\cdot(\pi/2)^{n}\cdot(2e)^{n}.

Consequently, for
R=2\pi e
and uniformly for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in\real
, we have

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|\psi^{(j)}(t)|}{(R)^{j}j!}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2^{j}}\leq 1.

Thus,
\psi\in\mathcal{F}(2\pi e)
. Finally, note that for all
t\in\real
, we have

\psi(t)-\psi(t/2)=\log\bigg(\frac{e^{\frac{\pi t}{2}}+e^{-\frac{\pi t}{2}}}{e^{\frac{\pi t}{4}}+e^{-\frac{\pi t}{4}}}\bigg)\geq\log(1/2).

Consequently, we obtain
\sup_{t\in\real}\big\{e^{-\psi(t)+\psi(t/2)}\big\}\leq 2
. One can use Faà di Bruno’s formula to evaluate
\psi^{(n)}(t)
, since there are at must
\mathcal{O}(e^{\pi\sqrt{n}})
terms in the summation and each term can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{poly}(n))
. Thus, the total computational time is
\mathcal{O}(e^{5\sqrt{n}})
.
\clubsuit

The proof for the logistic distribution is identical so we omit it.

6.5
Proof of Corollary
2

We must verify the assumption, bound the error quantities, and prove the consequence; we do so separately.

Verifying Assumption
2
.

By definition, we have

\displaystyle\psi^{(j)}(t)=\begin{cases}\beta!\cdot t^{\beta-j}/(\beta-j)!\;\;&\text{ if }1\leq j\leq\beta\\
0\;\;&\text{ if }j\geq\beta+1.\end{cases}

We thus obtain for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in[n]
that

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|\psi^{(j)}(t)|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)^{k}\leq\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\frac{\binom{\beta}{j}|t|^{\beta-j}}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\big)^{j}}\bigg)^{k}\leq\beta^{k-1}\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\bigg(\frac{\beta}{\widetilde{C}_{\psi}}\bigg)^{jk}|t|^{(\beta-j)k}\leq\frac{1}{2\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}|t|^{(\beta-j)k},

where in the last step we set
\widetilde{C}_{\psi}=2\beta^{2}
. Integrating both sides over
t\in\real
yields

\displaystyle\int e^{-\psi(t)}\cdot\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|\psi^{(j)}(t)|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{\psi}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)^{k}\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{1}{2\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\int e^{-\psi(t)}|t|^{(\beta-j)k}\mathrm{d}t\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}k!\leq n!.

In step
{\sf(i)}
we use that for any
j\in[\beta]
,

\displaystyle\int e^{-\psi(t)}|t|^{(\beta-j)k}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta}{\Gamma(1/\beta)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-t^{\beta}}t^{(\beta-j)k}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{\Gamma(1/\beta)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-t^{\beta}}\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\beta}{\Gamma(1/\beta)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-t^{\beta}}t^{\beta k}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1/\beta)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-x}x^{1/\beta-1}\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{\Gamma(1/\beta)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-x}x^{k+1/\beta-1}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\Gamma(k+1/\beta)}{\Gamma(1/\beta)}=1+k!\leq 2\cdot(k!)

Thus, Assumption
2
holds with
\widetilde{C}_{\psi}=2\beta^{2}
and
C_{\psi}=1
.

Bounding
\mathsf{Err}(\psi,n,\lambda)
and
M(\psi,n,\lambda)
.

Note that for
|t|\leq\lambda^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}
and
\lambda\geq 4\beta^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|\psi^{(j)}(t)|}{\lambda^{j}j!}=\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\frac{\binom{\beta}{j}|t|^{\beta-j}}{\lambda^{j}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\frac{\beta^{j}\lambda^{1/2}}{\lambda^{j}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\frac{1}{2^{j}}\leq 1.

Consequently, we obtain that for
\lambda\geq 4\beta^{2}
and for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Big\{t\in\real:|t|\leq\lambda^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}\Big\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\psi,n,\lambda)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,n,\lambda)\subseteq\Big\{t\in\real:|t|\geq\lambda^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}\Big\}.

We now turn to bound
\mathsf{Err}(\psi,n,\lambda)
(
25
). Using the relation in the display above, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,n,\lambda)}e^{-\psi(t)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{\Gamma(1/\beta)}\int_{\lambda^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}}^{+\infty}e^{-t^{\beta}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta}{\Gamma(1/\beta)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\big(t+\lambda^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}\big)^{\beta}}\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{\beta}{\Gamma(1/\beta)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-t^{\beta}}\mathrm{d}t\cdot e^{-\sqrt{\lambda}}=e^{-\sqrt{\lambda}}.

(97)

Continuing, we have that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,n,\lambda)}e^{-\psi(t)}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\frac{|\psi^{(j)}(t)|}{\lambda^{j}j!}\bigg)^{k}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{A}^{\complement}(\psi,n,\lambda)}e^{-\psi(t)}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\frac{\beta^{j}|t|^{\beta-j}}{\lambda^{j}}\bigg)^{k}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{\Gamma(1/\beta)}\int_{\lambda^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}}^{+\infty}e^{-t^{\beta}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\frac{\beta^{j}|t|^{\beta-j}}{\lambda^{j}}\bigg)^{k}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}\frac{\beta}{\Gamma(1/\beta)}\int_{\lambda^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}}^{+\infty}e^{-t^{\beta}}\frac{t^{\beta k}}{\lambda^{k/2}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}e^{-\sqrt{\lambda}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{\beta}{\Gamma(1/\beta)}e^{-\frac{t^{\beta}}{2}}\mathrm{d}t\leq 2e^{-\sqrt{\lambda}}.

(98)

Step
{\sf(i)}
above holds for all
\lambda\geq 4\beta^{2}
. In this case, for
|t|\geq\lambda^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}\geq 1
, we have

\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\frac{\beta^{j}|t|^{-j}}{\lambda^{j/2}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\frac{1}{2^{j}}\leq 1.

Step
{\sf(ii)}
above holds for all
\lambda\geq 4k^{2}
; in this case

\displaystyle\int_{\lambda^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}}^{+\infty}e^{-t^{\beta}}t^{\beta k}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\geq\lambda^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}}\Big\{t^{\beta k}e^{-t^{\beta}/2}\Big\}\cdot\int_{\lambda^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}}^{+\infty}e^{-t^{\beta}/2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{k/2}e^{-\sqrt{\lambda}/2}\cdot\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-t^{\beta}/2}\mathrm{d}t\cdot e^{-\sqrt{\lambda}/2}=\lambda^{k/2}e^{-\sqrt{\lambda}}\cdot\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-t^{\beta}/2}\mathrm{d}t.

Combining Ineq. (
6.5
) and Ineq. (
6.5
) yields that

\mathsf{Err}(\psi,n,\lambda)\leq 3e^{-\sqrt{\lambda}}\quad\text{for all }\lambda\geq 4n^{2}\vee 4\beta^{2}\text{ and }n\in\mathbb{N}.

We next bound
M(\psi,n,\lambda)
(
26
). Since
-\psi(t)+\psi(t/2)\leq-t^{\beta}/2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\real}\bigg\{e^{-\psi(t)+\psi(t/2)}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|\psi^{(j)}(t)|}{\lambda^{j}j!}\bigg)^{k}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq\beta^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\frac{\beta^{jk}}{\lambda^{jk}}\cdot\sup_{t\in\real}\bigg\{e^{-\frac{t^{\beta}}{2}}\cdot|t|^{(\beta-j)k}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}\beta^{k}k^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\bigg(\frac{\beta}{\lambda}\bigg)^{jk}\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}\frac{\beta^{2k}k^{k}}{\lambda^{k}}\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\frac{1}{2^{j-1}}\overset{{\sf(iii)}}{\leq}2.

In step
{\sf(i)}
above, we use the chain of bounds

\sup_{t\in\real}\bigg\{e^{-\frac{t^{\beta}}{2}}\cdot|t|^{(\beta-j)k}\bigg\}\leq 1\vee\sup_{t>1}\Big\{e^{-\frac{t^{\beta}}{2}}\cdot|t|^{\beta k}\Big\}\leq 1\vee k^{k}\leq k^{k}.

Step
{\sf(ii)}
above holds for all
\lambda\geq\beta^{2}
, since
\beta\geq 2
. Finally, step
{\sf(iii)}
holds for all
\lambda\geq\beta^{2}n
. Putting together the pieces, we obtain by definition (
26
) that

M(\psi,n,\lambda)\leq 3,\quad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }\lambda\geq\beta^{2}n.

Proof of the consequence.

Note that since
C_{\psi}=1
, the requirement on
N
from conditions (
27
) and (
29
) is
N\geq C\log^{4}(C/\epsilon)
for a universal and positive constant
C
. It suffices to set
\lambda\geq 4\beta^{2}N
to satisfy the conditions from the previous parts. We thus have

\mathsf{Err}(\psi,2N,\lambda)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{6}\quad\text{and}\quad M(\psi,2N,\lambda)\leq 3,\quad\text{if}\quad\lambda\gtrsim\beta^{2}\log^{8}(C/\epsilon).

Consequently, by letting
T_{\mathsf{iter}}\gtrsim\log(3/\epsilon)
, we obtain from guarantee (
30
) that

\sup_{\theta\in\real}\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\;(\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta}),Y_{\theta})\leq\epsilon.

Note that the eventual requirement on
\tau
from condition (
27
) reads as
\tau\gtrsim\sigma\beta^{2}\log^{12}(C/\epsilon)
.

We now turn to the computational complexity of the reduction. Note that we can use Eq. (
6.1
) to evaluate the signed kernel
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}
(
14
). Since
\psi^{(j)}(t)=0
for
j\geq\beta+1
, then the summation in Eq. (
6.1
) is over all tuples of nonnegative integers
(m_{1},\cdots,m_{\beta})
satisfying
\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}jm_{j}=n
. There are at most
n^{\beta}
such tuples. Thus, evaluating
\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)
takes time
\mathcal{O}(n^{\beta+1})
. Since in addition we have
N=\mathcal{O}(\log^{4}(C/\epsilon))
, we can bound the evaluation time
T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N)=\mathcal{O}(\log^{4\beta+4}(C/\epsilon))
. We view the sampling time as a constant (
T_{\mathsf{samp}}=\mathcal{O}(1)
), since the generalized normal distribution is straightforward to sample from. Putting this together with number of iterations
T_{\mathsf{iter}}\asymp\log(3/\epsilon)
yields a total computational complexity of
\mathcal{O}\Big(\log^{4\beta+5}(C/\epsilon)\Big)
.
∎

7
Proofs of results from Section
2.3

We prove Theorem
2
(a) in Section
7.1
and Theorem
2
(b) in Section
7.2
.
We provide the proof of the corollaries and concrete examples of Theorem
2
in Sections
7.3
–
7.5
.

7.1
Proof of Theorem
2
(a)

Throughout this section, to reduce the notational burden, we use the shorthand:

\displaystyle h_{j}(x)=\frac{f^{(j)}(x/\tau)}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{j}j!},\quad\phi(x)=\frac{e^{-x^{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}},\quad g(x)=\frac{f(x/\tau)-y}{2},\quad\text{for all }x,y\in\real,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

(99)

We first state an auxiliary lemma, whose proof we defer to Appendix
B.3
.

Lemma 5
.

Let
v(y;\theta)
be defined as in Eq. (
33
). Let
h_{j}(x)
,
\phi(x)
, and
g(x)
be defined in Eq. (
99
).
Then we have for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
x,y\in\real
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}^{(n)}v(y;\theta)\big|_{\theta=x}=\frac{(\widetilde{C}_{f})^{n}}{\tau^{n}}\cdot n!\cdot\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{(-1)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}\cdot\phi(g(x))\cdot H_{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}(g(x))}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{h_{j}(x)}{2}\bigg)^{m_{j}},

(100)

where the summation is over all
n
-tuples of nonnegative integers
(m_{1},\cdots,m_{n})
satisfying
\sum_{j=1}^{n}jm_{j}=n
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\nabla_{\theta}^{(n)}v(y;\theta)\big|_{\theta=x}\Big|\leq e^{-\frac{g(x)^{2}}{2}}\cdot\frac{(2\widetilde{C}_{f})^{n}}{\tau^{n}}\cdot n!\cdot e^{n}\cdot\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}|h_{j}(x)|\bigg)^{n}\vee 1\bigg].

(101)

We now use the Lemma
5
to verify that the target density
v(y;\theta)
defined in Eq. (
33
) satisfies Assumption
1
, ensuring that Lemma
1
is applicable.

Part (a) of the assumption immediately holds by definition of
v(y;\theta)
(
33
).
We now verify Assumption
1
(b). Ineq. (
101
) implies that

\displaystyle\int\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)\big|e^{-\frac{(x-\theta)^{2}}{2}}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(2e\widetilde{C}_{f})^{n}}{\tau^{n}}\cdot n!\cdot\bigg[\int\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}|h_{j}(x)|\bigg)^{n}e^{-\frac{(x-\theta)^{2}}{2}}\mathrm{d}x+\int e^{-\frac{(x-\theta)^{2}}{2}}\mathrm{d}x\bigg]

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{=}\frac{(2e\widetilde{C}_{f})^{n}}{\tau^{n}}\cdot n!\cdot\bigg[\sqrt{2}\int\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|f^{(j)}((\theta+\sqrt{2}t)/\tau)|}{(\widetilde{C}_{f})^{j}j!}\bigg)^{n}e^{-t^{2}}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\pi}\bigg]

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}\frac{(2e\widetilde{C}_{f})^{n}}{\tau^{n}}\cdot n!\cdot\Big(2\sqrt{\pi}\cdot(C_{f}n)!+\sqrt{2\pi}\Big)<+\infty,

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use the change of variable
t=(x-\theta)/\sqrt{2}
, and in step
{\sf(ii)}
we use Assumption
3
. Note that
\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)\big|e^{-\frac{(x-\theta)^{2}}{2}}
is a continuous and nonnegative function on , and its integral is bounded. This means it converges to zero at infinity, that is,

\lim_{x\uparrow+\infty}\big[\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)\big]e^{-\frac{(x-\theta)^{2}}{2}}=\lim_{x\downarrow-\infty}\big[\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)\big]e^{-\frac{(x-\theta)^{2}}{2}}=0.

This proves Assumption
1
(b).
We now verify Assumption
1
(c). Note that by definition (
33
),
v(y;\sqrt{2}x+\theta)\leq 1/\sqrt{\pi}
for all
x,y,\theta\in\real
, and consequently

\int\Big(v\big(y;\sqrt{2}x+\theta\big)\Big)^{2}\exp(-x^{2})\mathrm{d}x\leq\int e^{-x^{2}}/\sqrt{\pi}\mathrm{d}x=1<+\infty.

This proves that Assumption
1
(c) holds.

Consequently, Lemma
1
holds and we obtain

\displaystyle\delta\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{T}^{\star}_{N}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\varsigma\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\int\big(|p(x)-1|+q(x)\big)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x.

(102)

To bound the terms appearing on the RHS above, we state an auxiliary lemma, whose proof we defer to the following subsections.

Lemma 6
.

Let the source and target statistical models
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
be given by Eq. (
2
) (with
\sigma=1
) and Eq. (
32
), respectively. Let the signed kernel
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}
be defined in Eq. (
14
) and let
\varsigma\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}\big)
be defined in Eq. (
10
). Suppose the pair of parameters
(N,\tau)
satisfies condition (
36
) and Assumption
3
holds. Let
\overline{C}_{f}
be defined in Eq. (
35
). Then the following statements hold:

(a)

We have

\displaystyle\varsigma\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}\big)\leq\overline{C}_{f}\exp\bigg(-(2e)^{-1}(2N+1)^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{f})}}\bigg).

(103)

(b)

Let
p(x)
and
q(x)
be defined in Eq. (
17
). Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\theta\in\real}\;\int\big(|p(x)-1|+q(x)\big)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\leq 12\exp(-\tau/8)+15\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\exp\bigg(\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}-\frac{\tau^{2}}{8}\bigg).

(104)

Substituting Ineq. (
103
) and Ineq. (
104
) into the RHS of Ineq. (
102
) proves part (a) of the theorem.
∎

We next prove Ineq. (
103
) in Section
7.1.1
, and prove Ineq. (
104
) in Section
7.1.2
.

7.1.1
Proof of Lemma
6
(a)

The proof is identical to that of Lemma
3
(a) in Section
6.1.1
.
We state two key claims. First, for all
\tau\geq 2\vee 2|\theta|\vee 2\widetilde{C}_{f}
and
n,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
y\in\real
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\ell}\big[v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)e^{-x^{2}}\big]}{\mathrm{d}x^{\ell}}\overline{H}_{n+1+\ell}(x)\bigg]\bigg|_{-\infty}^{+\infty}=0,

(105)

where the evaluation at infinity is with respect to variable
x
. Second, for all
\tau\geq 8\sqrt{2}e\widetilde{C}_{f}
and for all
r,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
k\leq r
, we have

\displaystyle\int\int\bigg|\nabla_{x}^{(r-k)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)\cdot e^{-x^{2}/2}\bigg|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\leq 4\sqrt{2}\pi(r-k)!\cdot\frac{1}{4^{r-k}}\cdot\big(C_{f}(r-k)\big)!.

(106)

We prove the two claims above, which are analogous to Claims (
75
) and (
78
) in Section
6.1.1
, and we omit the remaining steps to prove the lemma, since these are exactly parallel to before.

Proof of Claim (
105
).

By definition, we have for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
that

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)=\nabla_{z}^{(n)}v\big(y;z\big)\big|_{z=\theta+\sqrt{2}x}\cdot(\sqrt{2})^{n}.

Applying Ineq. (
101
), we obtain

\displaystyle\big|\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n/2}\cdot\Big|\nabla_{z}^{(n)}v\big(y;z\big)\big|_{z=\theta+\sqrt{2}x}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{g(\theta+\sqrt{2}x)^{2}}{2}}\cdot\frac{(2\sqrt{2}e\widetilde{C}_{f})^{n}}{\tau^{n}}\cdot n!\cdot\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big|h_{j}\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)\big|\bigg)^{n}\vee 1\bigg]

(107)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(2\sqrt{2}e\widetilde{C}_{f})^{n}}{\tau^{n}}\cdot n!\cdot\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big|h_{j}\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)\big|\bigg)^{n}\vee 1\bigg].

(108)

Using the product rule (
6.1.1
) and Ineq. (
62a
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\ell}\big[v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)e^{-x^{2}}\big]}{\mathrm{d}x^{\ell}}

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{n+1+\ell}(x)\bigg|\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{k}\Big|\nabla_{x}^{(\ell-k)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)\cdot H_{k}(x)e^{-x^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{k}\frac{(2\sqrt{2}e\widetilde{C}_{f})^{\ell-k}}{\tau^{\ell-k}}\cdot(\ell-k)!\cdot\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-k}\big|h_{j}\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)\big|\bigg)^{\ell-k}\vee 1\bigg]\cdot\sqrt{\gamma_{k}}\cdot e^{-x^{2}/2},

where in the last step we have used Ineq. (
108
) and
|H_{k}(x)e^{-x^{2}/2}|\leq\sqrt{\gamma_{k}}
with
\gamma_{k}=\sqrt{\pi}2^{k}k!
from Ineq. (
62a
). Note that from Assumption
3
and Eq. (
99
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-k}\big|h_{j}\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)\big|\bigg)^{\ell-k}e^{-x^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}x\leq\sqrt{2\pi}(C_{f}(\ell-k))!<+\infty.

If the integral of a continuous and nonnegative function over is finite, then it must converge to zero at infinity. Thus, for all
\ell-k\geq 0

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-k}\big|h_{j}\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)\big|\bigg)^{\ell-k}e^{-x^{2}/2}=0.

Combining the pieces yields

\displaystyle\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{\ell}\big[v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)e^{-x^{2}}\big]}{\mathrm{d}x^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{H}_{n+1+\ell}(x)=0,

which proves Claim (
105
).

Proof of Claim (
106
).

Applying Ineq. (
107
) yields

\displaystyle\int\int\bigg|\nabla_{x}^{(r-k)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)\cdot e^{-x^{2}/2}\bigg|\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\big(2\sqrt{2}e\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{r-k}}{\tau^{r-k}}\cdot(r-k)!\cdot\int\int e^{-\frac{g(\theta+\sqrt{2}x)^{2}}{2}}\mathrm{d}y\cdot\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{r-k}\big|h_{j}\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)\big|\bigg)^{r-k}+1\bigg]e^{-x^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}x.

Recall the function
g(x)=(f(x/\tau)-y)/2
defined in Eq. (
99
). We obtain

\int e^{-\frac{g(\theta+\sqrt{2}x)^{2}}{2}}\mathrm{d}y=\int e^{-y^{2}/4}\mathrm{d}y=2\sqrt{\pi}.

By Assumption
3
and by definition of
h_{j}(x)
in Eq. (
99
), we obtain

\int\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{r-k}\big|h_{j}\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)\big|\bigg)^{r-k}+1\bigg]e^{-x^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}x\leq\sqrt{2\pi}\big(1+(C_{f}(r-k))!\big)\leq 2\sqrt{2\pi}\cdot(C_{f}(r-k))!.

Combining all the pieces and letting
\tau\geq 8\sqrt{2}e\widetilde{C}_{f}
, we have

\int\int\bigg|\nabla_{x}^{(r-k)}v\big(y;\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)\cdot e^{-x^{2}/2}\bigg|\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{4\sqrt{2}\pi}{4^{r-k}}\cdot(C_{f}(r-k))!\cdot(r-k)!.

This concludes the proof of Claim. (
106
).
∎

7.1.2
Proof of Lemma
6
(b)

We first state an auxiliary lemma and defer its proof to Appendix
B.4
.

Lemma 7
.

Consider the signed kernel
\mathcal{S}^{\star}_{N}(y\mid x)
defined in Eq. (
14
) for target density
v(y;\theta)
defined in Eq. (
33
). For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
L_{f}(n)
be defined as in Eq. (
35
). Recall the shorthand in Eq. (
99
). Suppose Assumption
3
holds with the pair of constants
(C_{f},\widetilde{C}_{f})
. Then we have:

(a)

If
\tau\geq 4e^{3}\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{2}\big(L_{f}(2N)\vee 1\big)N
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\geq 0\quad\text{for all}\quad|x|\leq\tau,\quad|y-f(x/\tau)|\leq\sqrt{\tau},\quad N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

(109)

(b)

For all
|x|\leq\tau
and
k\geq 1
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\int_{f(\frac{x}{\tau})+\sqrt{\tau}}^{+\infty}\big|\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\big|\mathrm{d}y+\int_{-\infty}^{f(\frac{x}{\tau})-\sqrt{\tau}}\big|\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\big|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{2\pi}\exp(-\tau/8)\cdot(2k)!\cdot\bigg(\frac{2e\widetilde{C}_{f}}{\tau}\bigg)^{2k}\cdot\big(L_{f}(2k)\vee 1\big)^{2k}.

(110)

(c)

For all
x\in\real
and
n\geq 1
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\int\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y=0.

(111)

Taking the above lemma as given, let us now prove Lemma
6
(b).
We split the proof into two cases.

Case 1: bound
|p(x)-1|+q(x)
for
|x|\leq\tau
.

By definition (
17
), we obtain for all
x\in\real

\displaystyle p(x)-q(x)

\displaystyle=\int\big[\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\vee 0\big]+\big[\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\wedge 0\big]\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(2k)!!}\int\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y\overset{{\sf(i)}}{=}1+0=1,

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use Eq. (
111
) to control the second term and definition (
33
) to control the first, whereby
\int v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y=\int e^{-y^{2}/2}/\sqrt{2\pi}\mathrm{d}y=1
. We consequently obtain
q(x)=p(x)-1
for all
x\in\real
. Moreover, note that since
q(x)\geq 0
by definition (
17
), we obtain
p(x)\geq 1
for all
x\in\real
. We thus obtain

\displaystyle|p(x)-1|+q(x)\leq 2(p(x)-1)\quad\text{for all }x\in\real.

(112)

We next turn to upper bound
p(x)
when
|x|\leq\tau
. Using Ineq. (
109
) and definition of
p(x)
(
17
), we have

\displaystyle p(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{f(\frac{x}{\tau})-\sqrt{\tau}}^{f(\frac{x}{\tau})+\sqrt{\tau}}\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\mathrm{d}y+\int_{f(\frac{x}{\tau})+\sqrt{\tau}}^{+\infty}\big|\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\big|\mathrm{d}y+\int_{-\infty}^{f(\frac{x}{\tau})-\sqrt{\tau}}\big|\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\big|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\int v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{(2k)!!}\int_{f(\frac{x}{\tau})-\sqrt{\tau}}^{f(\frac{x}{\tau})+\sqrt{\tau}}\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{(2k)!!}\bigg(\int_{f(\frac{x}{\tau})+\sqrt{\tau}}^{+\infty}\big|\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\big|\mathrm{d}y+\int_{-\infty}^{f(\frac{x}{\tau})-\sqrt{\tau}}\big|\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\big|\mathrm{d}y\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 1+2\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{(2k)!!}\bigg(\int_{f(\frac{x}{\tau})+\sqrt{\tau}}^{+\infty}\big|\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\big|\mathrm{d}y+\int_{-\infty}^{f(\frac{x}{\tau})-\sqrt{\tau}}\big|\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\big|\mathrm{d}y\bigg)/

(113)

Here in the last step we use
\int v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y=1
by definition (
33
). We also and use Eq. (
111
) so that for
k\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{f(\frac{x}{\tau})-\sqrt{\tau}}^{f(\frac{x}{\tau})+\sqrt{\tau}}\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=-\int_{f(\frac{x}{\tau})+\sqrt{\tau}}^{+\infty}\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y-\int_{-\infty}^{f(\frac{x}{\tau})-\sqrt{\tau}}\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\int_{f(\frac{x}{\tau})+\sqrt{\tau}}^{+\infty}\big|\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\big|\mathrm{d}y+\int_{-\infty}^{f(\frac{x}{\tau})-\sqrt{\tau}}\big|\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\big|\mathrm{d}y.

Substituting Ineq. (
(b)
) into the RHS of Ineq. (
7.1.2
) yields

\displaystyle p(x)

\displaystyle\leq 1+4\sqrt{2\pi}e^{-\tau/8}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{(2k)!}{(2k)!!}\cdot(4e^{2})^{k}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\widetilde{C}_{f}}{\tau}\bigg)^{2k}\cdot\big(L_{f}(2k)\vee 1\big)^{2k}

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}1+4\sqrt{2\pi}e^{-\tau/8}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\bigg(\frac{8ke^{2}\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{2}\big(L_{f}(2k)\vee 1\big)^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\bigg)^{k}

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}1+4\sqrt{2\pi}e^{-\tau/8}\sum_{k=1}^{N}2^{-k}\leq 1+12e^{-\tau/8}.

(114)

Above, in step
{\sf(i)}
we use
(2k)!/(2k)!!=(2k-1)!!\leq(2k)^{k}
and in step
{\sf(ii)}
we use

\displaystyle\tau^{2}\geq 16Ne^{2}\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{2}\big(L_{f}(2N)\vee 1\big)^{2}\geq 16ke^{2}\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{2}\big(L_{f}(2k)\vee 1\big)^{2},\quad\text{for all }k\in[N].

Combining Ineq. (
7.1.2
) with Ineq. (
112
) yields

\displaystyle\big|p(x)-1\big|+q(x)\leq 2\big(p(x)-1\big)\leq 24\exp(-\tau/8),\quad\text{for all }|x|\leq\tau.

(115)

This concludes the proof for the first case. Now we consider the second case.

Case 2: bound
|p(x)-1|+q(x)
for
|x|\geq\tau
.

By definition, we obtain

\displaystyle p(x)\vee q(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int\big|\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\big|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{(2k)!!}\int\big|\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\big|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}1+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{(2k)!}{(2k)!!}\cdot(4e^{2})^{k}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\widetilde{C}_{f}}{\tau}\bigg)^{2k}\cdot\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\big|h_{j}(x)\big|\bigg)\vee 1\bigg]^{2k}\int e^{-t^{2}/8}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}1+2\sqrt{2\pi}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\bigg(\frac{8e^{2}k\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\bigg)^{k}\cdot\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\big|h_{j}(x)\big|\bigg)\vee 1\bigg]^{2k}.

In step
{\sf(i)}
we use Ineq. (
101
) and note that since
g(x)=\frac{f(x/\tau)-y}{2}
, we also use

\int e^{-\frac{g(x)^{2}}{2}}\mathrm{d}y=\int e^{-\frac{(y-f(x/\tau))^{2}}{8}}\mathrm{d}y=\int e^{-t^{2}/8}\mathrm{d}t,

where in the last step we use change of variables
t=y-f(x/\tau)
. In step
{\sf(ii)}
, we use the inequalities
(2k)!/(2k)!!=(2k-1)!!\leq(2k)^{k}
and
\int e^{-t^{2}/8}\mathrm{d}t\leq 2\sqrt{2\pi}
. Note that
p(x),q(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in\real
by definition (
17
). Consequently, we obtain

\displaystyle|p(x)-1|+q(x)

\displaystyle\leq 1+p(x)+q(x)

\displaystyle\leq 3+4\sqrt{2\pi}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\bigg(\frac{8e^{2}k\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\bigg)^{k}\cdot\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\big|h_{j}(x)\big|\bigg)\vee 1\bigg]^{2k},\quad\text{for all }x\in\real.

(116)

Putting the two cases together.

We have

\displaystyle\int\big(|p(x)-1|+q(x)\big)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|x|\leq\tau}\big(|p(x)-1|+q(x)\big)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{|x|\geq\tau}\big(|p(x)-1|+q(x)\big)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x.

We then bound the two integrals in the display above. Applying Ineq. (
115
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{|x|\leq\tau}\big(|p(x)-1|+q(x)\big)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\leq 24\exp(-\tau/8)\cdot\int_{|x|\leq\tau}u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\leq 24\exp(-\tau/8).

(117)

Applying Ineq. (
7.1.2
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{|x|\geq\tau}\big(|p(x)-1|+q(x)\big)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 3\int_{|x|\geq\tau}u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x+4\sqrt{2\pi}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\bigg(\frac{8e^{2}k\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\bigg)^{k}\cdot\int_{|x|\geq\tau}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\big|h_{j}(x)\big|\bigg)\vee 1\bigg]^{2k}\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x.

(118)

By definition of
u(x;\theta)
(
4
) for
\sigma=1
, we obtain

u(x;\theta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(x-\theta)^{2}}{2}}\leq e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{4}},

where in the last step we use
(x-\theta)^{2}\geq x^{2}+\theta^{2}-2|\theta x|\geq x^{2}+\theta^{2}-(x^{2}/2+2\theta^{2})\geq x^{2}/2-\theta^{2}
. Consequently, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{|x|\geq\tau}u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\leq 2e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}}\int_{\tau}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{4}}\mathrm{d}x\leq\sqrt{2}e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}}e^{-\frac{\tau^{2}}{4}},

(119)

and similarly for
k\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{|x|\geq\tau}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\big|h_{j}(x)\big|\bigg)\vee 1\bigg]^{2k}\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}}\int_{|x|\geq\tau}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\big|h_{j}(x)\big|\bigg)\vee 1\bigg]^{2k}\cdot\frac{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{4}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}}\int_{|x|\geq\tau}\frac{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{8}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{d}x\cdot\Big(\widetilde{L}_{f}(2k,\tau)\vee 1\Big)^{2k}

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}2e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}}e^{-\frac{\tau^{2}}{8}}\cdot\Big(\widetilde{L}_{f}(2k,\tau)\vee 1\Big)^{2k}.

(120)

Above, in step
{\sf(i)}
we use definition of
h_{j}(x)
(
99
) and
\widetilde{L}_{f}(n,\tau,\sigma)
(
35
) so that for all
|x|\geq\tau
,

\displaystyle\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\big|h_{j}(x)\big|\bigg)\vee 1\bigg]e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{8(2k)}}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\frac{|f(x/\tau)|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)\vee 1\bigg]e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{8(2k)}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{|t|\geq 1}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\frac{|f(t)|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)\vee 1\bigg]e^{-\frac{\tau^{2}t^{2}}{8(2k)}}

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{L}_{f}(2k,\tau)\vee 1.

In step
{\sf(ii)}
, we use

\int_{|x|\geq\tau}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{8}}\mathrm{d}x\leq 2\exp\big(-\tau^{2}/8\big).

Substituting Ineq. (
119
) and Ineq. (
7.1.2
) into Ineq. (
7.1.2
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{|x|\geq\tau}\big(|p(x)-1|+q(x)\big)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}}e^{-\frac{\tau^{2}}{8}}\cdot\bigg(3+4\sqrt{2\pi}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Bigg(\frac{8e^{2}k\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\bigg)^{k}\cdot\Big(\widetilde{L}_{f}(2k,\tau)\vee 1\Big)^{2k}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}2e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}}e^{-\frac{\tau^{2}}{8}}\cdot\bigg(3+4\sqrt{2\pi}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{2^{k}}\Bigg)\leq 30e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}}e^{-\frac{\tau^{2}}{8}},

(121)

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use the condition

\tau^{2}\geq 16e^{2}N\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{2}\big(\widetilde{L}_{f}(2N,\tau)\vee 1\big)^{2}\geq 8e^{2}k\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{2}\big(\widetilde{L}_{f}(2k,\tau)\vee 1\big)^{2}\quad\text{for all }k\in[N].

Finally, combining Ineq. (
7.1.2
) with Ineq. (
117
) yields

\displaystyle\int\big(|p(x)-1|+q(x)\big)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\leq 24\exp(-\tau/8)+30e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}}e^{-\frac{\tau^{2}}{8}}.

Taking a supremum over
\theta\in\Theta
proves the desired result. ∎

7.2
Proof of Theorem
2
(b)

We split the proof into three parts.

Construction of the reduction based on rejection kernel.

We define a class of base measures
\left\{\mathcal{D}(\cdot\mid x)\right\}_{x\in\mathbb{R}}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(y\mid x)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-f(x/\tau))^{2}}{8}\right),\quad\text{for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}.

(122)

As before, we use the general rejection kernel
x\mapsto\textsc{rk}(x,T,M,y_{0})
from
Lou et al. 2025
with the base measures
\mathcal{D}(y\mid x)
defined in (
122
) and the signed kernel
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)
defined in (
14
).
As before,
M>0
is a constant that is required to satisfy: for input
x
,

\frac{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\vee 0}{\mathcal{D}(y\mid x)}\leq M,\quad\text{for all }y\in\mathbb{R},

and
y_{0}
is the initialization. Throughout this section, we set

\displaystyle T=\left\lceil 4\sqrt{2\pi}\log(4/\epsilon)\right\rceil,\quad M=2\sqrt{2\pi},\quad\text{and}\quad y_{0}=0.

(123)

We are now ready to describe the algorithm
\mathsf{K}
. For
X_{\theta}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta,\sigma^{2})
, define

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta}):=\begin{cases}\textsc{rk}(X_{\theta},T,M,y_{0}),&\text{if }|X_{\theta}|\leq\tau,\\
y_{0},&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(124)

Note that for all
|x|\leq\tau
and
y\in\real
, we have by definition of
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)
(
14
),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\vee 0}{\mathcal{D}(y\mid x)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{1}{(2k)!!}\frac{\big|\nabla_{x}^{(2k)}v(y;x)\big|}{\mathcal{D}(y\mid x)}

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}2\sqrt{2\pi}\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(2k)!}{(2k)!!}\cdot\frac{(4e^{2})^{k}\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{2k}}{\tau^{2k}}\cdot\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\big|h_{j}(x)\big|\bigg)\vee 1\bigg]^{2k}

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\leq}2\sqrt{2\pi}\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(2k)^{k}\cdot(4e^{2})^{k}\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{2k}}{\tau^{2k}}\cdot\big(L_{f}(2k)\vee 1\big)^{2k}

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(iii)}}{\leq}2\sqrt{2\pi}\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{1}{2^{k}}=M.

In step
{\sf(i)}
we use Ineq. (
B.4
) and definition of
\mathcal{D}(y\mid x)
(
122
). In step
{\sf(ii)}
we use
(2k)!/(2k)!!=(2k-1)!!\leq(2k)^{k}
and recall
h_{j}(x)
(
99
) and
L_{f}(2k)
(
35
), so that

\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\big|h_{j}(x)\big|\leq L_{f}(2k)\quad\text{for all }|x|\leq\tau.

In step
{\sf(iii)}
we use condition (
36
) that
\tau
is large enough to guarantee that the summation is bounded. Therefore, the reduction
\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})=\textsc{rk}(X_{\theta},T,M,y_{0})
when
|X_{\theta}|\leq\tau
(
124
) is well defined.

Computational complexity of the reduction.

The rejection kernel
x\mapsto\textsc{rk}(x,T,M,y_{0})
requires, at each iteration, generating a sample from the uniform distribution
\mathsf{Unif}([0,1])
and a sample from the Gaussian base measure (
122
), both of which take
\mathcal{O}(1)
time. Additionally, it requires evaluating the signed kernel
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}
once per iteration, which takes time
T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N)
. The procedure is repeated for at most
T=\mathcal{O}(\log(4/\epsilon))
iterations. Therefore, the total computational complexity of the reduction
\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})
is

\mathcal{O}\big(\log(4/\epsilon)\cdot T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N)\big).

Proof of TV deficiency guarantee of the reduction.

Having defined the reduction, we turn to prove the claimed guarantee (
39
). Applying the triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta}),Y_{\theta}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\big\|\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})-v(\cdot;\theta)\big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})-\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\right\|_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x-v(\cdot;\theta)\right\|_{1}.

Taking the supremum over
\theta\in\Theta
on both sides yields

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta}),Y_{\theta}\big)\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\left\|\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})-\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\right\|_{1}+\delta\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}\big).

(125)

We claim that under our setting of parameters (
38
) (
123
), the following holds.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\left\|\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})-\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2},

(126a)

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\delta\big(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V};\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}\big)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

(126b)

The desired guarantee Ineq. (
39
) follows immediately. We now prove the above two inequalities separately.

Proof of Ineq. (
126a
).

For each
x
satisfying
|x|\leq\tau
, we let
Y=\textsc{rk}(x,T,M,y_{0})
. Then from
Lou et al. 2025
, we obtain that the random variable
Y
has the conditional distribution

\displaystyle f_{Y}(y\mid x)=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\vee 0}{p(x)}\cdot\big(1-(1-p(x)/M)^{T}\big)+\delta_{y_{0}}(y)\cdot(1-p(x)/M)^{T},\quad\text{for all }y\in\real,

(127)

where
p(x)
is defined in Eq. (
17
) and
\delta_{y_{0}}(y)
is the Dirac delta function centered at
y_{0}
. By the law of total probability and definition of
\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})
in Eq. (
124
), we obtain that
\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})
has the marginal distribution

\displaystyle f_{\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{|x|\leq\tau}f_{Y}(y\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x+\delta_{y_{0}}(y)\cdot\int_{|x|\geq\tau}u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{|x|\leq\tau}\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\cdot(1-\ell(x))\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\delta_{y_{0}}(y)\cdot\bigg(\int_{|x|\geq\tau}u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{|x|\leq\tau}\ell(x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\bigg),

(128)

where in the last step we have used Eq. (
127
) along with the shorthand notation
\ell(x)=(1-p(x)/M)^{T}
and the previously defined notation for the Markov kernel (
15
),
\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\vee 0}{p(x)}
. Further recall from Ineq. (
7.1.2
) that
|p(x)-1|\leq 12\exp(-\tau/8)\leq\epsilon/2\leq 1/2
for all
|x|\leq\tau
. Consequently, we obtain that for all
|x|\leq\tau
,

\displaystyle 1/2\leq p(x)\leq 3/2\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\ell(x)\leq e^{-p(x)T/M}\leq e^{-T/(2M)}.

(129)

Using Eq. (
7.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg\|\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})-\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\bigg\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\int\bigg|\int_{|x|\leq\tau}\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\cdot(1-\ell(x))\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x-\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\int_{|x|\geq\tau}u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{|x|\leq\tau}\ell(x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int\int_{|x|\leq\tau}\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\cdot\ell(x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\int\int_{|x|\geq\tau}\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\int_{|x|\geq\tau}u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{|x|\leq\tau}\ell(x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x,

where in the last step we apply the triangle inequality. Continuing, we bound each of the terms in the display above

\displaystyle\int\int_{|x|\leq\tau}\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\cdot\ell(x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{|x|\leq\tau}\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\mathrm{d}y\cdot\ell(x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{|x|\leq\tau}\ell(x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\leq e^{-\frac{T}{2M}},

where in the last step we use Ineq. (
129
). Also, we have

\int\int_{|x|\geq\tau}\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)\cdot u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\int_{|x|\geq\tau}u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\leq e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}}\int_{|x|\geq\tau}\frac{e^{-x^{2}/(4)}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{d}x\leq\sqrt{2}e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}-\frac{\tau^{2}}{4}}.

Combining the three pieces together yields

\displaystyle\bigg\|\mathsf{K}(X_{\theta})-\int\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\star}(\cdot\mid x)u(x;\theta)\mathrm{d}x\bigg\|_{1}\leq 2e^{-\frac{T}{2M}}+2\sqrt{2}e^{\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}-\frac{\tau^{2}}{4}}\leq\epsilon,

where in the last step we use
T\geq 2M\log(4/\epsilon)
and
\tau^{2}\geq 4\log(4\sqrt{2}/\epsilon)+2\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\theta^{2}
. This concludes the proof of Ineq. (
126a
).

Proof of Ineq. (
126b
).

Since we set

2N+1\geq\Big(2e\log\big(4\overline{C}_{f}/\epsilon\big)\Big)^{2(1+C_{f})},\quad\tau^{2}\geq 64\log^{2}\big(96/\epsilon\big)\vee\;\Big[8\log\big(120/\epsilon\big)+4\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\theta^{2}\Big],

we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{f}\exp\bigg(-(2e)^{-1}(2N+1)^{\frac{1}{2(1+C_{f})}}\bigg)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle 12\exp(-\tau/8)+15\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\exp\bigg(\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}-\frac{\tau^{2}}{8}\bigg)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}.

Substituting the two inequalities in the display above into Ineq. (
37
) completes the proof.
∎

7.3
Proof of Corollary
3

We verify each part separately.

Verifying Assumption
3
.

By definition, we obtain
f^{(j)}(x)=B!\cdot x^{B-j}/(B-j)!
for all
x\in\real
and
0\leq j\leq B
, and
f^{(j)}(x)=0
for all
x\in\real
and
j\geq B+1
. We now verify Assumption
3
in two cases.

Case 1:
n\leq B
.

For
n\leq B
and
x\in\real
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\big|f^{(j)}\big(\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)/\tau\big)\big|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{j}j!}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{B!}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{j}j!\cdot(B-j)!}\cdot\frac{\big|\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big|^{B-j}}{\tau^{B-j}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\binom{B}{j}}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{j}}\cdot\frac{(2|\theta|)^{B-j}+(2\sqrt{2}|x|)^{B-j}}{\tau^{B-j}}

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1+|x|^{B-j}}{(4e)^{j}},

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use
\binom{B}{j}\leq B^{j}
and let
\widetilde{C}_{f}=4eB
, and we use
\tau\geq 2\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}|\theta|
and
\tau\geq 2\sqrt{2}
. Consequently, we obtain for all
x\in\real
,

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\big|f^{(j)}\big(\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)/\tau\big)\big|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)^{n}\leq\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1+|x|^{B-j}}{(4e)^{j}}\bigg)^{n}

\displaystyle\leq n^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{1+|x|^{B-j}}{(4e)^{j}}\bigg)^{n}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n}n^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1+|x|^{(B-j)n}}{(4e)^{jn}}.

(130)

Integrating over
x
yields

\displaystyle\int\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\big|f^{(j)}\big(\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)/\tau\big)\big|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)^{n}\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{n}n^{n}}{(2e)^{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big[2^{-j}+2^{-j}\big((B-j)n!!\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}n!\cdot(2Bn)!\leq\big((2B+1)n\big)!,

(131)

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use
n^{n}\leq e^{n}n!
. Thus, Assumption
3
holds with
C_{f}=2B+1
and
C_{f}=4eB
for all
n\leq B
.

Case 2:
n\geq B+1
.
Note that
f^{(j)}(x)=0
for all
x\in\real
when
j\geq B+1
. Consequently, following the same steps as in Ineq. (
7.3
) yields

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\big|f^{(j)}\big(\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)/\tau\big)\big|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)^{n}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{B}\frac{\big|f^{(j)}\big(\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)/\tau\big)\big|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{j}j!}\bigg)^{n}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n}B^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{B}\frac{1+|x|^{(B-j)n}}{(4e)^{jn}}.

Integrating over
x
and using
B\leq n
yields the same bound as Ineq. (
7.3
).

Bounding the quantities
L_{f}(n)
and
\widetilde{L}_{f}(n,\tau)
.

We next turn to bound
L_{f}(n)
and
\widetilde{L}_{f}(n,\tau)
defined in Eq. (
35
). We obtain by definition that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle L_{f}(n)\leq\sup_{|x|\leq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{B}\frac{B!\cdot|x|^{B-j}}{(B-j)!\cdot\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{j}j!}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{B}(2e)^{-j}\leq 1,

where we use
\frac{B!}{(B-j)!\cdot j!}=\binom{B}{j}\leq B^{j}
and
\widetilde{C}_{f}=2eB
. Reasoning similarly, we obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{f}(n,\tau)\leq\sup_{|x|\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{B}\frac{|x|^{B}}{(2e)^{j}}\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{\tau^{2}x^{2}}{8n}\bigg)\leq\sup_{|x|\geq 1}|x|^{B}\exp\bigg(-\frac{\tau^{2}x^{2}}{8n}\bigg)\leq 1,

where in the last step we let
\tau^{2}\geq 8nB
, whereby the supremum is achieved at
|x|=1
.

Bounding the evaluation time
T_{\mathsf{eval}}(n)
.

We next bound the evaluation time
T_{\mathsf{eval}}(n)
of the signed kernel
\mathsf{S}_{n}^{\star}(y\mid x)
(
14
),
noting the closed-form expression (
100
). Note that
h_{j}(x)=0
for all
j\geq B+1
and
x\in\real
. Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)

\displaystyle=n!\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{B}}\frac{(-1)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{B}}}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{B}!}\cdot H_{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{B}}(g(x))\phi(g(x))\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{B}\bigg(\frac{h_{j}(x)}{2}\bigg)^{m_{j}}.

Here the summation is over all
B
-tuples of nonnegative integers
(m_{1},\cdots,m_{B})
satisfying
\sum_{j=1}^{B}jm_{j}=n
; thus there is at most
n^{B}
such
B
-tuples. Also, each term in the summation can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
using Eq. (
62c
).
Consequently, evaluating
\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)
for any pair of
(x,y)
takes time
\mathcal{O}(n^{B+2})
. Using the expression (
14
), we conclude that
T_{\mathsf{eval}}(n)=\mathcal{O}(n^{B+2})
.

Proof of the consequence.

Finally, note that conditions (
36
) and (
38
) are equivalent to

N\geq C_{B}\log^{4(B+1)}(C_{B}/\epsilon)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau^{2}\geq C_{B}\cdot\log^{8(B+1)}(C_{B}/\epsilon)+4\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\theta^{2},

where
C_{B}
is a constant that depends only on
B
. Applying Theorem
2
with the guarantees above yields the desired result.
∎

7.4
Proof of Corollary
4

We verify each part separately.

Verifying Assumption
3
.

Let
\widetilde{C}_{f}=2R
. Since
f\in\mathcal{F}(R)
, then we obtain for all
x\in\real
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\big|f^{(j)}\big(\big(\theta+\sqrt{2}x\big)/\tau\big)\big|}{\big(\widetilde{C}_{f}\big)^{j}j!}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2^{j}}\leq 1.

Consequently, Assumption
3
holds with
\widetilde{C}_{f}=2R
and
C_{f}=0
.

Bounding the quantities
L_{f}(n)
and
\widetilde{L}_{f}(n,\tau)
.

We obtain by definition (
35
),

\displaystyle L_{f}(n)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2^{j}}\leq 1\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{L}_{f}(n,\tau)\leq 1\quad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N},\tau>0.

Bounding the evaluation time
T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N)
.

We first derive the computational time of evaluating
\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)
. Using Faà di Bruno’s formula yields

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}^{(n)}v(y;x)

\displaystyle=n!\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{(-1)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}\cdot H_{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}(g(x))\phi(g(x))\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{f^{(j)}(x/\tau)}{\tau^{j}j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}},

where the summation is over all nonnegative integers
(m_{1},\dots,m_{n})
satisfying
\sum_{j=1}^{n}jm_{j}=n
. Let
P(n)
denote the number of
n
-tuples of nonnegative integers
(m_{1},\cdots,m_{n})
satisfying
\sum_{j=1}^{n}jm_{j}=n
. Note that
P(n)
corresponds to the partition function and asymptotically satisfies

P(n)\sim\frac{1}{4n\sqrt{3}}\exp\Big(\pi\sqrt{2n/3}\Big)\quad\text{as}\quad n\uparrow+\infty.

Thus, there are at most
\mathcal{O}\big(\exp\big(\pi\sqrt{n}\big)\big)
terms in the above summation, and each term in the summation can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{poly}(n)\exp\big(C\sqrt{n}\big)\big)
provided we can evaluate
f^{(n)}(x)
in time
\mathcal{O}\big(\exp\big(C\sqrt{n}\big)\big)
, which is in turn true by assumption. Thus we can evaluate
\mathcal{S}_{N}^{\star}(y\mid x)
(
14
) in time

\mathcal{O}\Big(\mathsf{poly}(N)\exp\Big((C+\pi)\sqrt{N}\Big)\Big)=\mathcal{O}\big(\mathsf{poly}(1/\epsilon)\big),

where in the last step we use the following logic: Since
C_{f}=0
, it suffices to take
N=\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}(1/\epsilon))
. Thus
N
satisfies conditions (
36
) and (
38
).

Proof of the consequence.

Finally, note that conditions (
36
) and (
38
) are equivalent to

N\geq\widetilde{C}\log^{2}(\widetilde{C}/\epsilon)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau^{2}\geq\widetilde{C}R^{2}\cdot\log^{2}(\widetilde{C}/\epsilon)+4\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\theta^{2},

where
\widetilde{C}
is a universal constant. Applying Theorem
2
with the guarantees above yields the desired result.
∎

7.5
Proofs of claims in Example
2

We perform derivations for the different functions separately.

Trigonometric links.

Consider
f(\theta)=\cos(a\theta+b)
. Clearly, we have
|f^{(n)}(x)|\leq|a|^{n}
for all
x\in\real
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus by letting
R=2|a|
, we have

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|f^{(j)}(x)|}{R^{j}j!}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2^{j}}\leq 1.

Consequently,
f\in\mathcal{F}(2|a|)
. The same holds when
f(\theta)=\sin(a\theta+b)
. This concludes the proof.

Function
f(\theta)=\mathsf{sech}(\theta)=\frac{2}{e^{\theta}+e^{-\theta}}
.

We let
g(x)=\frac{2}{x}
and
h(x)=e^{x}+e^{-x}
. Note that
f(x)=g(h(x))
. Applying Faà di Bruno’s formula yields

\displaystyle f^{(n)}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{n!}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}g^{(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n})}(h(x))\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{h^{(j)}(x)}{j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}}

\displaystyle=n!\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}\frac{(-1)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}\cdot\frac{2(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n})!}{h(x)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{h^{(j)}(x)}{j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}}.

(132)

Note that by definition
|h^{(j)}(x)|\leq h(x)
for all
x\in\real
. Consequently, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n})!}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}\cdot\frac{1}{h(x)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{|h^{(j)}(x)|}{j!}\bigg)^{m_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n})!}{m_{1}!\,m_{2}!\cdots m_{n}!}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{j!}\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{j!}\bigg)^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}\leq 2^{n},

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use the multinomial theorem and in the last step we use
m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}\leq n
. Putting the pieces together and applying the triangle inequality yields for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\real
,

\displaystyle|f^{(n)}(x)|\leq 2n!\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}2^{n}\leq 2\cdot n!\cdot(2e)^{n},

where in the last step we use that there are at most
e^{n}
terms in the summation. Consequently, by letting
R=8e
, we obtain

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|f^{(j)}(x)|}{R^{j}j!}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}2^{-j}\leq 1.

Note that one can evaluate
f^{(n)}(x)
using Eq. (
7.5
), where one need to sum over at most
\mathcal{O}\Big(\exp\Big(\pi\sqrt{2n/3}\Big)\Big)
terms and each term in the summation can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{poly}(n))
. Thus, the evaluation time of
f^{(n)}(x)
can be bounded by

\mathcal{O}\Big(\mathsf{poly}(n)\cdot\exp\Big(\pi\sqrt{2n/3}\Big)\Big)=\mathcal{O}\Big(\exp\Big(\pi\sqrt{n}\Big)\Big).

Other functions.

For
f(\theta)=(1+\theta^{2})^{-1}
and
f(\theta)=(1+e^{-\theta})^{-1}
. we can follow the identical steps as above to show
f\in\mathcal{F}(8e)
. For
f(\theta)=\exp(-\theta^{2})
, we obtain

f^{(n)}(x)=(-1)^{n}H_{n}(x)\exp(-x^{2}).

By using the properties of Hermite polynomials (
62a
), it is straightforward to show
f\in\mathcal{F}(8e)
. We omit the details for brevity. ∎

8
Proofs of results from Section
3

In this section, we prove all the results related to the applications presented in this paper.

8.1
Proof of Proposition
2

We first state a result which directly follows by combining Theorem 10 and Lemma 102 of
Brennan and Bresler 2020
.

Proposition 4
.

Let
s\geq 3
be a constant. Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}=\beta(\sqrt{n}\widetilde{v})^{\otimes s}+\widetilde{\zeta},\quad\text{where }\widetilde{v}\sim\mathsf{Unif}\big(\big\{\tfrac{-1}{\sqrt{n}},\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big\}^{n}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\zeta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes n^{\otimes s}}.

(133)

Suppose the
k
-
\mathrm{HPC}^{s}
conjecture holds. If
\beta=\tilde{o}\big(n^{-s/4}\big)
and
\beta=\omega\big(n^{-s/2}\sqrt{s\log(n)}\big)
, then there exists
no
algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:\real^{n^{\otimes s}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
that runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\big(\widetilde{T}\big),\widetilde{v}\big\rangle=\Omega(1)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad\Omega_{n}(1)

We next present an auxiliary lemma establishing that there exists a polynomial-time reduction that maps
\widetilde{T}
defined in Eq. (
133
) to the tensor
T
defined in Eq. (
43
) and achieves zero TV deficiency.

Lemma 8
.

Let
\widetilde{T}
denote the tensor defined in Eq. (
133
) with signal strength
\beta\in\real
and spike
\sqrt{n}\widetilde{v}
. Then there exists a reduction
\mathcal{R}:\real^{n^{\otimes s}}\rightarrow\real^{n^{\otimes s}}
that runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\mathcal{R}(\widetilde{T})=\beta\big(\sqrt{n}U\widetilde{v}\big)^{\otimes s}+\zeta,

where
U
is an
n\times n
unitary matrix chosen uniformly at random and
\zeta
is as defined in Eq. (
42
).

We provide the proof Lemma
8
in Appendix
B.5
.
Note that
U\widetilde{v}\sim\mathsf{Unif}\big(\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\big)
and thus
\mathcal{R}\big(\widetilde{T}\big)
is equal in distribution to the tensor
T
defined in Eq. (
43
).

We now prove Proposition
2
by combining Proposition
4
with Lemma
8
. We prove it by contradiction. Let
T
be defined as in Eq. (
43
) and assume that there is an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\real^{n^{\otimes s}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
that runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathcal{A}\big(T\big),v\big\rangle=\Omega(1)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad\Omega_{n}(1).

Then the algorithm
\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{R}:\real^{n^{\otimes s}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{R}\big(\widetilde{T}\big),U\widetilde{v}\big\rangle=\Omega(1)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad\Omega_{n}(1).

This implies that

\displaystyle\big\langle U^{\top}\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{R}\big(\widetilde{T}\big),\widetilde{v}\big\rangle=\Omega(1)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad\Omega_{n}(1).

In words, the algorithm
U^{\top}\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{R}:\real^{n^{\otimes s}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and produces accurate recovery. However, this violates Proposition
4
. Consequently, our assumption that algorithm
\mathcal{A}
runs in polynomial time must be false, thereby proving Proposition
2
.
∎

8.2
Proof of Theorem
3

We partition the set of all index tuples

\big\{(i_{1},\dots,i_{s})\;\big|\;i_{k}\in[n],\;\forall\,k\in[s]\big\}

into disjoint subsets
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{L}
, where each subset
S_{\ell}
consists of index tuples that are equivalent up to permutation—that is, any two tuples in
S_{k}
are permutations of each other. Let
\mathsf{K}:\real\rightarrow\real
be the reduction in Theorem
1
. We design the tensor-to-tensor reduction algorithm
\mathcal{R}:\real^{n^{\otimes s}}\rightarrow\real^{n^{\otimes s}}
as follows.

Input:
T\in\real^{n^{\otimes s}}
. For each
\ell=1,2,\dots,L
:

1.

Pick one index tuple
\big(i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}\big)\in S_{\ell}
. Let
\mathcal{R}(T)_{i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}}=\mathsf{K}\big(T_{i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}}\big)
.

2.

For all other index tuples
(i^{\prime}_{1},\dots,i^{\prime}_{s})\in S_{\ell}\setminus\big\{\big(i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}\big)\big\}
, set
\mathcal{R}(T)_{(i^{\prime}_{1},\dots,i^{\prime}_{s})}=\mathcal{R}(T)_{i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}}
to ensure the output is a symmetric tensor.

We next bound the TV distance between
\mathcal{R}(T(\beta,v))
and
\widetilde{T}(\beta,v)
, where we use
T(\beta,v)
to denote the tensor from model (
41
) when
\beta
and
v
are the fixed parameters, and likewise
\widetilde{T}(\beta,v)
is the tensor from model (
45
). Recall the density of
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
in Eq. (
46
) and the density of
\mathcal{Q}_{\theta}
in Eq. (
20
). Conditional on
\beta
and
v
, we have by definition for all
\ell\in[L]
that

T(\beta,v)_{i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta,\sigma_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}^{2})\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{T}(\beta,v)_{i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}}\sim\mathcal{Q}_{\theta},\quad\text{where }\theta=\beta n^{s/2}\times v_{i_{1}^{\ell}}\times v_{i_{2}^{\ell}}\times\cdots\times v_{i_{s}^{\ell}}.

Note that
\sigma_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}
is a constant, since
s
is a fixed parameter and
1/s!\leq\sigma_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}^{2}\leq 1
.
Using Assumption
4
and Theorem
1
, we then obtain our reduction. In particular, for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, if
\tau=\Theta\big(\mathsf{polylog}(1/\epsilon)\big)
then setting
T_{\mathsf{iter}}=\Theta\big(\log(1/\epsilon)\big)
yields, for all
\ell\in[L]
, that

\sup_{\beta\in\real,v\in\real^{n}}\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{K}\big(T(\beta,v)_{i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}}\big),\widetilde{T}(\beta,v)_{i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}}\Big)\leq\epsilon.

Applying the triangle inequality for the TV distance (or equivalently, a union bound)
(
Brennan et al. 2018
, Lemma 6)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathcal{R}\big(T(\beta,v)\big),\widetilde{T}(\beta,v)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathcal{R}\big(T(\beta,v)\big)_{i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}},\widetilde{T}(\beta,v)_{i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{K}\big(T(\beta,v)_{i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}}\big),\widetilde{T}(\beta,v)_{i_{1}^{\ell},\dots,i_{s}^{\ell}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq L\cdot\epsilon\leq n^{s}\cdot\epsilon.

For each
\delta\in(0,1)
, let
\epsilon=\delta/n^{s}
. Then taking the supremum over
\beta\in\real
and
v\in\real^{n}
of the inequality in the display above completes the proof of Theorem
3
. Note that from Assumption
4
and Theorem
1
, the reduction algorithm
\mathcal{R}
runs in time

\mathcal{O}\Big(n^{s}\cdot T_{\mathsf{samp}}\cdot T_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N)\Big)=\mathcal{O}\Big(\mathsf{poly}\big(n^{s}/\delta\big)\Big),

where
T_{\mathsf{samp}}
is independent of
\delta
and
n
.
∎

8.3
Proof of Corollary
5

Let
T
be a tensor defined as in Eq. (
43
) and let
\widetilde{T}
be the tensor defined in Theorem
5
. Note that
T
and
\widetilde{T}
both have the same signal strength
\beta\in\real
and spike
v\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
. Let
\mathcal{R}
be the reduction defined in Proposition
3
with TV deficiency
\delta=n^{-1}
. By Proposition
3
, we have that
\mathcal{R}
runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies
\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathcal{R}(T),\widetilde{T}\big)\leq n^{-1}
. We now prove Corollary
5
by contradiction. Assume that there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\real^{n^{\otimes s}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
that runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\big\langle\mathcal{A}\big(\widetilde{T}\big),v\big\rangle=\Omega(1)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad\Omega_{n}(1).

Then
\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{R}:\real^{n^{\otimes s}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
also runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\big\langle\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{R}\big(T\big),v\big\rangle=\Omega(1)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad\Omega_{n}(1)-n^{-1}.

However, this contradicts Proposition
2
. Thus, our assumption that the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
runs in polynomial time cannot hold, thereby proving Corollary
5
.
∎

8.4
Proof of Theorem
4

Let
\mathsf{K}:\real\rightarrow\real
be the reduction in Theorem
2
.
We design the reduction algorithm
\mathcal{R}:(\real^{d}\times\real)^{n}\rightarrow(\real^{d}\times\real)^{n}
as follows: For inputs
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
from mixtures of linear regressions, return

\mathcal{R}\big(\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\big):=\{(x_{i},\mathsf{K}(y_{i}))\}_{i=1}^{n}
. Note that by definition, conditioned on
x_{i}
and
R_{i}
,

y_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta,1)\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{y}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(f(\theta/\tau),1)\quad\text{where }\theta=R_{i}\cdot\langle x_{i},v\rangle.

Consequently, applying Assumption
5
and Theorem
2
, we obtain that for each
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,

\text{if}\quad\tau=\mathsf{polylog}(1/\epsilon)+2\max_{i\in[n]}|\langle x_{i},v\rangle|,\quad\text{then}\quad\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathsf{K}(y_{i}),\widetilde{y}_{i}\big)\leq\epsilon.

Applying the triangle inequality for the TV distance
(
Brennan et al. 2018
, Lemma 6)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathcal{R}\big(\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\big),\{(x_{i},\widetilde{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\big\{\big(x_{i},\mathsf{K}(y_{i})\big)\big\}_{i=1}^{n},\{(x_{i},\widetilde{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathsf{K}(y_{i}),\widetilde{y}_{i}\big)\leq n\epsilon.

The desired TV guarantee follows by letting
\epsilon=\delta/n
. From Assumption
5
and Theorem
2
, the total time taken to compute
\mathcal{R}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\Big(n\cdot\log(4n/\delta)\cdot T_{\mathsf{eval}}(N)\Big)=\mathcal{O}\Big(\mathsf{poly}(n/\delta)\Big),

as claimed.
∎

8.5
Proof of Corollary
6

We define the following event

\mathcal{E}=\Big\{\max_{i\in[n]}\big|\langle x_{i},v\rangle\big|\leq 10\log^{2}(n)\Big\}.

Note that
\langle x_{i},v\rangle\overset{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\eta^{2})
since
\|v\|_{2}=\eta
and
x_{i}\overset{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. Moreover, by the condition that
k=o(n^{1/6})
and
n=\tilde{o}(k^{2}/\eta^{4})
, we obtain
\eta=\widetilde{o}(k^{-1})\leq 1
. Consequently, applying a standard bound on Gaussian maxima yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}\big]\geq 1-n^{-3}.

(134)

By setting
\tau=\mathsf{polylog}(n/\delta)
for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, the reduction
\mathcal{R}
in Proposition
4
runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n/\delta)
. The TV-deficiency guarantee in Proposition
4
requires condition (
51
), which holds with high probability according to Ineq. (
134
). We now combine the high probability guarantee (
134
) with Proposition
4
to derive the desired TV-deficiency guarantee.

Let
\mathsf{K}:\real\rightarrow\real
be the reduction in Theorem
2
and note that

y_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(R_{i}\cdot\langle x_{i},v\rangle,1\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{y}_{i}\mid x_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(f\big(\langle x_{i},v\rangle/\tau\big),1\big).

To reduce the notational burden, we let
\rho:\real^{d}\rightarrow\real
denote the density function of
x_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, let
\gamma(\cdot;x),\widetilde{\gamma}(\cdot;x):\real\rightarrow\real
denote the conditional densities of
\mathsf{K}(y_{i})\mid x_{i}=x
and
\widetilde{y}_{i}\mid x_{i}=x
, respectively. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{R}\big(\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\big),\{(x_{i},\widetilde{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\leq}n\cdot\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\big((x_{i},\mathsf{K}(y_{i})),(x_{i},\widetilde{y_{i}})\big)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{n}{2}\int_{\real{}^{d}}\rho(x)\cdot\int\Big|\gamma(t;x)-\widetilde{\gamma}(t;x)\Big|\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{n}{2}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\rho(x)\cdot\int\Big|\gamma(t;x)-\widetilde{\gamma}(t;x)\Big|\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{n}{2}\int_{\mathcal{E}^{\complement}}\rho(x)\cdot\int\Big|\gamma(t;x)-\widetilde{\gamma}(t;x)\Big|\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x,

where step
{\sf(i)}
follows since the samples are
\mathsf{i.i.d.}
Towards bounding the two terms in the display above, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{n}{2}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\rho(x)\cdot\int\Big|\gamma(t;x)-\widetilde{\gamma}(t;x)\Big|\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x\leq n\cdot\int_{\mathcal{E}}\rho(x)\mathrm{d}x\cdot\frac{\delta}{n}\leq\delta,

where we use the guarantee that on event
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathsf{K}(y_{i}),\widetilde{y_{i}})\leq\delta/n
. Moreover, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{n}{2}\int_{\mathcal{E}^{\complement}}\rho(x)\cdot\int\Big|\gamma(t;x)-\widetilde{\gamma}(t;x)\Big|\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x\leq n\cdot\int_{\mathcal{E}^{\complement}}\rho(x)\mathrm{d}x\leq n\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big[\mathcal{E}^{\complement}\Big]\leq n^{-2}.

Putting the three pieces together and setting
\delta=n^{-1}
yields

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathcal{R}\big(\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\big),\{(x_{i},\widetilde{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\Big)\leq n^{-1}+n^{-2}\leq 2n^{-1}.

(135)

For the sake of contradiction, we assume that there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:(\real^{d}\times\real)^{n}\rightarrow\real^{d}
that runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and for sample size
n=\tilde{o}\big(k^{2}/\eta^{4}\big)
, satisfies

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{A}\big(\{x_{i},\widetilde{y}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\big)-v\big\|_{2}=o(\eta)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad 1-o_{n}(1).

(136)

Then we note that
\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{R}:(\real^{d}\times\real)^{n}\rightarrow\real^{d}
also runs in polynomial time and it is an estimation algorithm for data
\{x_{i},y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
from
\mathsf{MixSLR}
(
47
). Moreover, the TV-deficiency guarantee (
135
) and the guarantee of
\mathcal{A}
(
136
) imply

\big\|\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{R}\big(\{x_{i},y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\big)-v\big\|_{2}=o(\eta)\quad\text{with probability at least}\quad 1-o_{n}(1)-2n^{-1}=1-o_{n}(1).

This contradicts the computational hardness claimed in Proposition
3
. Thus, our assumption that algorithm
\mathcal{A}
runs in polynomial time must be false, thereby proving Corollary
6
.
∎

8.6
Proof of Theorem
5

We construct the reduction
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
using the scalar reduction
\mathsf{K}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
developed in Corollary
3
with the parameter setting
B=2
.

Input:
A matrix
M=\mu rc^{\top}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes n\times n}
for
r,c\in S_{n,k}
.

Procedure:
Construct a new matrix
\widetilde{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
:
For all
i,j\in[n]
, set
\widetilde{M}_{ij}=\mathsf{K}(M_{ij})
.

Output:
The reduced matrix
\mathcal{R}(M):=\widetilde{M}
.

We next bound the TV deficiency of the reduction
\mathcal{R}
. For notational convenience, we let
f:\real\rightarrow\real
be the square function, i.e.,
f(t)=t^{2}
for all
t\in\real
and let

\overline{M}:=\frac{\mu^{2}}{\tau^{2}k}|r||c|^{\top}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes n\times n},

where
|r|,|c|\in\real^{n}
denotes the vectors obtained by taking the entrywise absolute value of
r
and
c
, respectively.
Note that for all
r,c\in S_{n,k}
, we have

r_{i}^{2}c_{j}^{2}=\frac{|r_{i}|\cdot|c_{j}|}{k}\quad\text{for all}\quad i,j\in[n].

Then observe that for all
i,j\in[n]
,

M_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu r_{i}c_{j},1)\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{M}_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}\Big(f\Big(\frac{\mu r_{i}c_{j}}{\tau}\Big),1\Big).

Consequently, since
\big(\mu r_{i}c_{j}\big)^{2}\leq\mu^{2}/k^{2}
, applying Corollary
3
with
\epsilon=\delta/n^{2}
for any
\delta\in(0,1)
and using the condition on
\tau
(
61
) yield the TV guarantee

\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{K}(M_{ij}),\overline{M}_{ij}\Big)\leq\frac{\delta}{n^{2}}\quad\text{for all }i,j\in[n].

Putting the pieces together yields

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathcal{R}(M),\overline{M}\Big)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{K}(M_{ij}),\overline{M}_{ij}\Big)\leq\delta.

Note that the bound in the display is independent of
r,c\in S_{n,k}
and
\mu\in\real
. Taking the supremum over
r,c\in S_{n,k}
and
\mu\in\real
on both sides yields the desired TV deficiency guarantee.
The computational complexity of the reduction
\mathcal{R}
follows from the computational complexity of the scalar reduction
\mathsf{K}
in Corollary
3
, and we use this reduction
n^{2}
times in total. ∎

Appendix

Appendix A
Supplementary results

We state the computational hardness conjectures and prove some auxiliary lemmas and claims in this section.

A.1
Explicit statements of hardness conjectures

In this section, we explicitly state the conjectures that are used in Section
3
.

Given two positive integers
m
and
n
, let
\mathcal{G}(m,n,1/2)
denote the distribution on bipartite graphs
G
with parts of size
m
and
n
wherein each edge between the two parts is included independently with probability
1/2
. Let
k_{m}\in[m]
and
k_{n}\in[n]
such that
k_{n}
divides
n
. Let
E=\{E_{1},\dots,E_{k_{n}}\}
be a partition of
[n]
into
k_{n}
parts, each of size
n/k_{n}
. We define the distribution

k\text{-}\mathrm{BPC}_{E}(m,n,k_{m},k_{n},1/2)

on bipartite graphs with left side
[m]
and right side
[n]
as follows:

1.

Sample
G\sim\mathcal{G}(m,n,1/2)
.

2.

Choose a subset
S\subseteq[m]
of size
k_{m}
uniformly at random.

3.

Choose a subset
T\subseteq[n]
of size
k_{n}
uniformly at random subject to the constraint

|T\cap E_{j}|=1\quad\text{for all }j\in[k_{n}].

4.

Plant the complete bipartite graph between
S
and
T
in the graph
G
, i.e. set all edges
\{(u,v):u\in S,v\in T\}
to be present.

The resulting distribution on
G
is denoted
k\text{-}\mathrm{BPC}_{E}(m,n,k_{m},k_{n},1/2)
. We define the following two hypotheses:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{0}:G\sim\mathcal{G}(m,n,1/2)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{H}_{1}:G\sim k\text{-}\mathrm{BPC}_{E}(m,n,k_{m},k_{n},1/2).

(137)

We now state the
k
-
\mathrm{BPC}
conjecture.

Conjecture 1

(k-BPC)
.

Let
m
and
n
be two positive integers and be polynomial in one another. Let
k_{m}\in[m]
and
k_{n}\in[n]
such that
k_{n}
divides
n
.
Let
E
be any partition of
[n]
into
k_{n}
parts, each of size
n/k_{n}
, and
E
is released. Let
\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}
be defined in Eq. (
137
). If

k_{m}=o(\sqrt{m})\qquad\text{and}\qquad k_{n}=o(\sqrt{n}),

then there cannot exist an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\{0,1\}^{m\times n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
that runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\Big\{\mathcal{A}(G)=1\Big\}+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\Big\{\mathcal{A}(G)=0\Big\}=1-\Omega_{n}(1).

Let
n
and
s
be non-negative integers with
s\geq 3
. Let
\mathcal{G}^{s}(n,1/2)
denote the distribution on
s
-uniform hypergraphs
G
with
n
vertices and each hyperedge is included independently with probability
1/2
. Let
k\in[n]
and
k
divides
n
. Let
E=\{E_{1},\dots,E_{k}\}
be a partition of
[n]
into
k
parts, each of size
n/k
. We define the distribution
k\text{-}\mathrm{HPC}_{E}^{s}(n,k,1/2)
as follows:

1.

Sample
G\sim\mathcal{G}^{s}(n,1/2)
.

2.

Choose a subset
S\subseteq[n]
of size
k
uniformly at random subject to the constraint

|S\cap E_{j}|=1\quad\text{for all }j\in[k].

3.

Plant the complete
s
-uniform hypergraph with vertex set
S
in the graph
G
, i.e., add all
\binom{k}{s}
hyperedges within
S
to
G
.

The resulting distribution on
G
is denoted
k\text{-}\mathrm{HPC}_{E}^{s}(n,k,1/2)
.
We define two hypotheses:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{0}:G\sim\mathcal{G}^{s}(n,1/2)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{H}_{1}:G\sim k\text{-}\mathrm{HPC}_{E}^{s}(n,k,1/2).

(138)

We now state the
k
-
\mathrm{HPC}^{s}
conjecture.

Conjecture 2

(k-HPC)
.

Let
n
be a positive integer and let
k\in[n]
divides
n
.
Let
E
be any partition of
[n]
into
k
parts, each of size
n/k
, and
E
is released. Let
\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}
be defined in Eq. (
138
). If
k=o(\sqrt{n})
, then there cannot exist an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\{0,1\}^{n^{s}}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
that runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
and satisfies

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\Big\{\mathcal{A}(G)=1\Big\}+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\Big\{\mathcal{A}(G)=0\Big\}=1-\Omega_{n}(1).

A.2
Inefficiency of the plug-in approach

We next show that a contraction of the signal-to-noise ratio (equivalently, an inflation of the variance) by a factor of order polynomially in
1/\epsilon
is necessary for the plug-in approach. To compare with the guarantee of Theorem
2
, we consider the source model
\mathcal{U}
and target model
\mathcal{V}
defined as

\mathcal{U}=(\real,\{\mathcal{N}(\theta,1)\}_{\theta\in\Theta})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{V}=\big(\real,\big\{\mathcal{N}\big(\theta^{2}/\tau^{2},1\big)\big\}_{\theta\in\Theta}\big)\quad\text{with}\quad\Theta=[-1,1].

Given
X_{\theta}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta,1)
, the plug-in approach returns the random variable

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}_{\mathsf{plugin}}(X_{\theta}):=\frac{X_{\theta}^{2}}{\tau^{2}}+G,\quad G\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(139)

In the above, the random variable
G
is independent of
X_{\theta}
. Operationally, the plug-in approach uses the single sample
X_{\theta}
as an estimator of the true mean
\theta
and attempts to approximate the random variable
Y_{\theta}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta^{2}/\tau^{2},1)
.

Lemma 9
.

For all positive signal-to-noise ratio parameters
\tau^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}_{\mathsf{plugin}}(X_{\theta}),Y_{\theta}\big)\geq\frac{(\sqrt{2}-1)e^{-3/2}}{2\pi}\cdot\min\{1,\tau^{-2}\}.

Consequently, it is necessary to take
\tau^{2}\gtrsim\epsilon^{-1}
to guarantee
\epsilon
-TV deficiency.

Note that Lemma
9
illustrates the inefficiency of the plug-in approach. In contrast, Theorem
2
and Corollary
3
show that it suffices to take
\tau^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{polylog}(1/\epsilon))
for the approach we proposed.

Proof.

By definition of TV, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}_{\mathsf{plugin}}(X_{\theta}),Y_{\theta}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\sup_{t\in\real}\;\Big|\mathbb{P}\big\{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}_{\mathsf{plugin}}(X_{\theta})\leq t\big\}-\mathbb{P}\big\{Y_{\theta}\leq t\big\}\Big|

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(i)}}{\geq}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\sup_{t\in\real}\;\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{\theta^{2}+2\theta Z+Z^{2}}{\tau^{2}}+G\leq t\bigg\}-\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{\theta^{2}}{\tau^{2}}+G\leq t\bigg\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf(ii)}}{\geq}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{Z^{2}}{\tau^{2}}+G\leq 0\bigg\}-\frac{1}{2}\bigg|

where in step
{\sf(i)}
we use that for
Z,G\overset{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, we have

X_{\theta}\overset{(d)}{=}\theta+Z,\quad Y_{\theta}\overset{(d)}{=}\frac{\theta^{2}}{\tau^{2}}+G,\quad\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}_{\mathsf{plugin}}(X_{\theta})\overset{(d)}{=}\frac{\theta^{2}+2\theta Z+Z^{2}}{\tau^{2}}+G,

and in step
{\sf(ii)}
we evaluate the supremum at
t=\theta^{2}/\tau^{2}
and
\theta=0
. Continuing, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\frac{Z^{2}}{\tau^{2}}+G\leq 0\bigg\}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}\bigg[\int_{-\infty}^{-Z^{2}/\tau^{2}}\frac{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{d}x\bigg]=\frac{1}{2}-{\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}\bigg[\int_{0}^{Z^{2}/\tau^{2}}\frac{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{d}x\bigg].

Putting the two pieces together yields

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{plugin}}(X_{\theta}),Y_{\theta}\big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}\bigg[\int_{0}^{Z^{2}/\tau^{2}}\frac{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{d}x\bigg]

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{t^{2}/\tau^{2}}\frac{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{d}x\;\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{1}^{\sqrt{2}}\frac{e^{-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{d}t\int_{0}^{1/\tau^{2}}\frac{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{d}x=\frac{(\sqrt{2}-1)e^{-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{1/\tau^{2}}\frac{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{d}x.

Continuing, we have

\int_{0}^{1/\tau^{2}}\frac{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{d}x\geq\begin{cases}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}},&\text{if }\tau^{2}\leq 1\\
